such that the cyclic shift
\mathbf{x}^{(j)}=(a^{(j)}_{i},b^{(j)}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
fulfills
b^{(j)}_{1}+\cdots+b^{(j)}_{i}\geq 0
for every
1\leq i<m
. It is then standard to obtain the desired results by an exchangeability argument ; we leave details to the reader.

The following lemma will allow, roughly speaking, to decouple the dependence between the random variables
(H_{i})
and
(B_{i})
. It is a particular case of
[
CL16
, Theorem 1.2]
. However, in our setting it is elementary, so we provide a proof.

Lemma 3.5
.

We have, for every integers
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{H}_{n_{1}}=n_{2},\overline{B}_{n_{1}}=n_{3}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet}_{n_{1}}=n_{2}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{n_{2}}=n_{1}+n_{3}\right).

Proof 3.6
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{H}_{n_{1}}=n_{2},\overline{B}_{n_{1}}=n_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\cdots+h_{n_{1}}=n_{2}\\
b_{1}+\cdots+b_{n_{1}}=n_{1}+n_{3}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\mu_{\bullet}(h_{i})\,\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{h_{i}}=b_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h_{1}+\cdots+h_{n_{1}}=n_{2}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\mu_{\bullet}(h_{i})\right)\left(\sum_{b_{1}+\cdots b_{n_{1}}=n_{1}+n_{3}}\prod_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{h_{i}}=b_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h_{1}+\cdots+h_{n_{1}}=n_{2}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\mu_{\bullet}(h_{i})\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{n_{2}}=n_{1}+n_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet}_{n_{1}}=n_{2}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{n_{2}}=n_{1}+n_{3}\right).

This completes the proof.

In particular, note that

\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{H}_{n_{\bullet}}=n_{\circ},\overline{B}_{n_{\bullet}}=-1\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet}_{n_{\bullet}}=n_{\circ}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{n_{\circ}}=n_{\bullet}-1\right).

By combining Lemmas
3.1
and
3.5
, we can express the probability that a two-type alternating random BGW tree has a given number of vertices of both types.
In view of future use, we also consider two-type alternating random BGW trees
with
with white root
(and offspring distributions
\mu_{\circ},\mu_{\bullet}
).
We keep the condition that colors should be alternating so that,
in this setting, all vertices at even heights are white and have offspring distribution
\mu_{\circ}
,
while vertices at odd heights are black and have offspring distribution
\mu_{\bullet}
.
Such a two-type alternating random BGW tree with white root will be denoted
\mathscr{T}^{\circ}
.
In contrast, we denote by
\mathscr{T}^{\bullet}
the two-type alternating random BGW tree where the root is black,
which was simply denoted by
\mathscr{T}
before.

Corollary 3.7
.

For every
n_{\bullet}\geq 1
and
n_{\circ}\geq 0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet}}\rvert=n_{\bullet},\,\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet}}\rvert=n_{\circ}\right)=\frac{1}{n_{\bullet}}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet}_{n_{\bullet}}=n_{\circ}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{n_{\circ}}=n_{\bullet}-1\right)

(13)

and

\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}}\rvert=n_{\bullet},\,\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}}\rvert=n_{\circ}\right)=\frac{1}{n_{\circ}}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet}_{n_{\bullet}}=n_{\circ}-1\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{n_{\circ}}=n_{\bullet}\right).

(14)

The identity (
) is an immediate consequence of Lemmas
3.1
and
3.5
, and (
) follows by symmetry. We will need (in the proof of the forthcoming Lemma
3.19
) an extension to forests with a fixed number of components : if
\mathscr{F}^{\circ}_{j}
denotes a collection of
j
i.i.d. BGW trees distributed as
\mathscr{T}^{\circ}
(with a white root), then

\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\circ}_{j}}\rvert=n_{\bullet},\,\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\circ}_{j}}\rvert=n_{\circ}\right)=\frac{j}{n_{\circ}}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet}_{n_{\bullet}}=n_{\circ}-j\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{n_{\circ}}=n_{\bullet}\right).

(15)

Also, if
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}_{j}
denotes a collection of
j
i.i.d. BGW trees distributed as
\mathscr{T}^{\bullet}
(with a black root), then

\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}_{j}}\rvert=n_{\bullet},\,\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}_{j}}\rvert=n_{\circ}\right)=\frac{j}{n_{\bullet}}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet}_{n_{\bullet}}=n_{\circ}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{n_{\circ}}=n_{\bullet}-j\right).

(16)

The proofs are similar, and we leave the details to the reader.

3.3.
A functional invariance principle for alternating BGW trees

It is now time to consider specific offspring distributions.
These distributions will be chosen of the form

\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(i)=a_{\bullet}^{n}\cdot(b_{\bullet}^{n})^{i}\cdot\frac{(i+1)^{i-1}}{i!}\quad(i\geq 0),\qquad\mu_{\circ}^{n}(i)=a_{\circ}^{n}\cdot(b_{\circ}^{n})^{i}\cdot\frac{(i+1)^{i-1}}{i!}\quad(i\geq 0).

(17)

Indeed, according to Proposition
2.11
,
alternating BGW trees with these distributions are connected to minimal factorizations
(for the moment, let us forget that, in Proposition
2.11
, the root has a different offspring distribution).
We start by specifying the choices of the parameters

a_{\bullet}^{n}
,
b_{\bullet}^{n}
,
a_{\circ}^{n}
and
b_{\circ}^{n}
.
(Note that the chosen parameters and hence the offspring distributions depend on
n
.)

Lemma 3.8
.

For every
n\geq 1
, fix
K_{n}\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
.

(i)

We may choose positive parameters
a_{\bullet}^{n},b_{\bullet}^{n},a_{\circ}^{n},b_{\circ}^{n}
in a unique way such that (
)
defines probability distributions with respective means

m^{n}_{\bullet}\,\coloneqq\,\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}i\cdot\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(i)=\frac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}},\qquad m^{n}_{\circ}\,\coloneqq\,\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}i\cdot\mu_{\circ}^{n}(i)=\frac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}.

(ii)

Assume that
K_{n}\rightarrow\infty
. If
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, then

(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}\sim\frac{K_{n}}{n},\quad b_{\bullet}^{n}\sim\frac{K_{n}}{n}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}\sim\big(\frac{n}{K_{n}}\big)^{3},

where
(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}
and
(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}
denote respectively the variance of
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
.
In particular, if
c>0
is fixed and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, then
(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}\sim\tfrac{c}{\sqrt{n}}
and
(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}\sim\frac{n^{3/2}}{c^{3}}
.

Proof 3.9
.

Let
F
be the power series defined by

F(z)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{(k+1)^{k-1}}{k!}z^{k}.

(18)

It is elementary to check that
G(z)=\frac{zF^{\prime}(z)}{F(z)}
defines a continuous increasing function on
[0,1/e)
and that
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0+}G(z)=0
,
\lim_{z\rightarrow 1/e-}G(z)=\infty
. One then chooses
b_{\bullet}^{n},b_{\circ}^{n}
such that respectively
G(b_{\bullet}^{n})=\frac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}}
,
G(b_{\circ}^{n})=\frac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}
, and then
a_{\bullet}^{n}=1/F(b_{\bullet}^{n})
,
a_{\circ}^{n}=1/F(b_{\circ}^{n})
.
This proves (i).

For (ii), since
G(z)=z+o(z)
as
z\rightarrow 0
and since
G(b_{\bullet}^{n})=\frac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}}
, we get that
b_{\bullet}^{n}\sim\frac{K_{n}}{n}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
The estimate for the variance
(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}
is then obtained by using the expression

(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}=\frac{(b_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}F^{\prime\prime}(b_{\bullet}^{n})}{F(b_{\bullet}^{n})}+m_{\bullet}^{n}-(m_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}
:

the dominant term when
n\rightarrow\infty
is
m_{\bullet}^{n}

and we have
(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}\sim\frac{K_{n}}{n}
.

The estimate concerning
(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}
is more subtle, as it involves the behavior of
F
near its radius of convergence
1/e
.
We can analytically extend
F

on a complex split neighborhood on
1/e
(i.e. on a complex neighborhood of
1/e
, without the real half-line
[1/e,+\infty)
).
Indeed we have
F(z)=-W(-z)/z
, where
W
is the Lambert function (see
[
CGH+96
, Eq 3.1]
).
Using
[
CGH+96
, Eq 4.22]
, as
z\rightarrow 0
, we have

F\left(\frac{1}{e}-z\right)=e-\sqrt{2}e^{3/2}\sqrt{z}+o(\sqrt{z}),

(19)

where
z
is a complex number avoiding the negative real line
(throughout the paper, we use the principal determination of

\sqrt{z}
when
z
is in
\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R}_{<0}
).
By singular differentiation
[
FS09
, Theorem VI.8 p. 419]
,
we get expansions for
F^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{1}{e}-z\big)
and
F^{\prime\prime}\big(\tfrac{1}{e}-z\big)

by differentiating the right-hand side of (
).
Hence
G(1/e-z)\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{2e}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}
as
z\rightarrow 0
.
By definition
b_{\circ}^{n}
is the solution of
G(b_{\circ}^{n})=\tfrac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}
, so that

1/e-b_{\circ}^{n}\quad\mathop{\sim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\frac{1}{2e}\left(\frac{K_{n}}{n-K_{n}}\right)^{2}\quad\mathop{\sim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\frac{1}{2e}\left(\frac{K_{n}}{n}\right)^{2}.

(20)

We again use the expression
(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}=\frac{(b_{\circ}^{n})^{2}F^{\prime\prime}(b_{\circ}^{n})}{F(b_{\circ}^{n})}+m_{\circ}-m_{\circ}^{2}

to estimate the variance.
An easy computation gives
(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}\sim({n}/{K_{n}})^{3}
.
(This time, the dominant term is
\frac{(b_{\circ}^{n})^{2}F^{\prime\prime}(b_{\circ}^{n})}{F(b_{\circ}^{n})}
.)

We denote by
\mathscr{T}_{n}
an alternating two-type BGW tree (with black root), with offspring distributions
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
conditioned on having
n-K_{n}
black vertices and
K_{n}+1
white vertices. Recall from the beginning of Section
3.2
the definition of the path
(B_{i}(\mathscr{T}_{n}))_{1\leq i\leq n-K_{n}}
.
We are aiming at a functional invariance theorem for a renormalized version of
(B_{i}(\mathscr{T}_{n}))_{1\leq i\leq n-K_{n}}
.
In the sequel,
X
,
X^{\mathrm{br}}
and
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
are as in Section
3.1
:

X
is the Lévy process with the specific characteristic exponent given in (
),
and
X^{\mathrm{br}}
and
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
are the associated bridge and excursion processes.

Instead of working as usual with
\mathbb{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
, we will work with
\mathbb{D}([-1,1],\mathbb{R})

by extending our function with value
0
on
[-1,0)
.
The reason for that is that our limiting process
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
almost surely
takes a positive value in
0
(it “starts with a jump”),
while
(\overline{B}_{i}(\mathscr{T}_{n}))_{1\leq i\leq n-K_{n}}
stays small for a small amount of time.
Formally, we set
X^{\mathrm{exc}}_{t}=0
for
t<0
,
and
\overline{B}^{n}_{i}(\mathscr{T}_{n})=0
for
i\leq 0
or
i>n-K_{n}
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c>0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. The following assertions hold.

(i)

The convergence

\left(c\frac{\overline{B}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\mathscr{T}_{n})}{n}:-1\leq u\leq 1\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(X^{\mathrm{exc}}_{u}:-1\leq u\leq 1)

holds in distribution.

(ii)

Let
\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{\bullet}
be the probability distribution defined by

\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{\bullet}(i)=\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(i-1)
for
i\geq 1

and consider a random tree
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
with distribution
{\textrm{BGW}}^{\widetilde{\mu}_{\bullet},\mu_{\bullet},\mu_{\circ}}
,
conditioned on having
n-K_{n}
black vertices and
K_{n}+1
white vertices
(the distribution
{\textrm{BGW}}^{\widetilde{\mu}_{\bullet},\mu_{\bullet},\mu_{\circ}}
is defined in (
) ; informally this is an alternating two-type BGW tree with black root, offspring distributions
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
except the root which has offspring distribution
\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{\bullet}
).
Then the convergence

\left(c\frac{\overline{B}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n})}{n}:-1\leq u\leq 1\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(X^{\mathrm{exc}}_{u}:-1\leq u\leq 1)

holds in distribution.

The main difficulty is (i). Indeed, in the forthcoming Lemma
3.19
(iii),
implies
\mathscr{T}_{n}
and
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
can be coupled so that
\mathscr{T}_{n}=\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}

with probability tending to
1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
(see e.g.
[
Lin92
]
).
Therefore, (ii) follows from (i).

From Lemma
3.1
,
(\overline{B}_{i}(\mathscr{T}_{n}))_{i\geq 1}
has the distribution of a conditioned random walk,
so that Theorem
3.10
(i) is in fact an invariance principle for a conditioned random walk.
Thanks to Lemma
3.3
,
we can consider a simpler conditioning of the form “bridge” instead of “excursion”.

To state the invariance principle with this simpler conditioning, let us
recall some notation from Section
3.2
, adding an exponent
n
to keep in mind that the
chosen offspring distributions do depend on
n
.
First, we denote by
S^{\bullet,n}
and
S^{\circ,n}
the random walks with respective jump distributions given by
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
.
Then we let
(H^{n}_{k},B^{n}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence of i.i.d. random variables with distribution
given by : for
i,j\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\big(H^{n}=i,B^{n}=j\big)=\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(i)\,\mathbb{P}\big(S^{\circ,n}_{i}=j\big).

Finally, for
i\geq 1
, we set
\overline{H}^{n}_{i}=H^{n}_{1}+H^{n}_{2}+\cdots+H^{n}_{i}
and
\overline{B}^{n}_{i}=B^{n}_{1}+B^{n}_{2}+\cdots+B^{n}_{i}-i
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c>0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Conditionally given the event

\{\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=K_{n}+1,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=-1\}
, the convergence

\left(c\cdot\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}}{n}\right)_{0\leq u\leq 1}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad X^{\mathrm{br}}

holds in distribution.

Before proving Proposition
3.11
, we explain how it implies Theorem
3.10
.

Proof 3.12

(Proof of Theorem
3.10
(i) using Proposition
3.11
)
.

Recall that
\mathcal{V}
denotes the Vervaat transform that has been introduced in the end of Section
3.1
. From Lemma
3.3
(ii) and Lemma
3.1
(ii), it is elementary to get that

\mathcal{V}\left(\left(\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}\right)_{{0\leq u\leq 1}}\right)\quad\mathop{=}^{(d)}\quad\left(\frac{\overline{B}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\mathscr{T}_{n})}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}\right)_{0\leq u\leq 1}.

Note that
X^{\mathrm{br}}
reaches its infimum at a unique time almost surely :
indeed, this is true for the unconditioned process
X
on every fixed interval, and transfers to
X^{\mathrm{br}}
by the absolute continuity relation (
).
Therefore

\mathcal{V}
is almost surely continuous at
X^{\mathrm{br}}
, and it follows that

\left(\frac{\overline{B}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\mathscr{T}_{n})}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}\right)_{-1\leq u\leq 1}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}^{(d)}\quad(X^{\mathrm{exc}}_{u})_{-1\leq u\leq 1}.

(21)

(Since
X^{\mathrm{br}}
jumps a.s. at its minimum,
\mathcal{V}
is not a.s. continuous at
X^{\mathrm{br}}
when seen as a functional

\mathbb{D}([0,1])\to\mathbb{D}([0,1])
, but only as a functional
\mathbb{D}([0,1])\to\mathbb{D}([-1,1])
;
details are left to the reader.)
This completes the proof.

Remark 3.13
.

It is possible to strengthen the previous results by considering a bivariate Vervaat transform and to establish that

\left(\frac{\overline{H}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\mathscr{T}_{n})-uK_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}},\frac{\overline{B}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\mathscr{T}_{n})}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}\right)_{-1\leq u\leq 1}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}^{(d)}\quad(W^{\mathrm{br}},X^{\mathrm{exc}}),

where
W^{\mathrm{br}}
is a Brownian bridge independent of
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
(with
W^{\mathrm{br}}_{u}=0
for
u<0
).
However, since we do not need this bivariate convergence for our initial goal (proving Theorem
1.3
(i)
c>0
),
we focus here on the convergence of the second component only.

The strategy of the proof of Proposition
3.11
consists of two steps : first we establish a convergence of the unconditioned processes (Lemma
3.16
), and then we show Proposition
3.11
by writing an analogue of the absolute continuity relation (
) in the discrete setting and by passing to the limit. We will heavily rely on the following uniform local limit estimates concerning the random walks
S^{\bullet,n}
and
S^{\circ,n}
(the proofs, very technical, are postponed to Section
3.5
).

Lemma 3.14
.

Fix
0<u\leq 1
. For
n,N\geq 1
, set
D^{\bullet}_{n,N}=\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}N}
. Assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow 0
and that
K_{n}\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
We have

\sup_{un\leq N\leq n}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|D^{\bullet}_{n,N}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{N}=k\right)-p\left(\frac{k-N\tfrac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}}}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}\right)\right|\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0,

where
p(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}
is the standard Gaussian density.

Lemma 3.15
.

Fix
u>0
. Assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c>0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. We set
D^{\circ}_{n}=\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}\sim n/c
.
Then it holds that

\sup_{|j|\leq n^{3/8}}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|D^{\circ}_{n}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{uK_{n}+j}=k\right)-q_{u}\left(\frac{k}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)\right|\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0,

where

q_{u}(x)=\left(\frac{u^{2}c^{3}}{2\pi x^{3}}\right)^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{c(x-uc)^{2}}{2x}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{x>0}.

(22)

We have chosen the exponent
3/8\in(1/4,1/2)
in view of specific future use, but as the proof will show we can replace
3/8
by any positive value less than
1/2
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c>0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Consider sequences
(\overline{H}^{n}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and
(\overline{B}^{n}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
as defined before Proposition
3.11
.
Without conditioning, the following convergence holds in distribution in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

\left(\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}-uK_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}},\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}\right)_{u\geq 0}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(W_{u},X_{u})_{u\geq 0},

where
W
is a standard Brownian motion, independent from the Lévy process
X
.

Aiming for Proposition
3.11
, the convergence of the first component seems to be superfluous.
However, its behavior has to be controlled in order to obtain a limit theorem for the second component
in conditioned setting.

Proof 3.17
.

In virtue of
[
Kal02
, Theorem 16.14]
, it is enough to check that the one-dimensional convergence holds for
u=1
. Recall that
p
is the density of the standard Gaussian distribution and that

q_{1}(x)=\left(\frac{c^{3}}{2\pi x^{3}}\right)^{1/2}e^{-\frac{c(x-c)^{2}}{2x}}\mathbbm{1}_{x>0},\qquad d_{1}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{c^{3}}{2\pi(x+c)^{3}}}\cdot e^{-\frac{cx^{2}}{2(x+c)}}\mathbbm{1}_{x\geq-c}

are respectively the density appearing in (
) and the density (
) of
X_{1}
. In particular, note that
q_{1}(x+c)=d_{1}(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

For fixed
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, by Lemma
3.5
, we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=\lfloor x\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}+K_{n}\rfloor,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=\lfloor y\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}\rfloor\big)\\
=\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n-K_{n}}=\lfloor x\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}+K_{n}\rfloor\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{\lfloor x\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}+K_{n}\rfloor}=n-K_{n}+\lfloor y\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}\rfloor\right).

Note that
\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}\sim\sqrt{c}\,n^{1/4}
. By the local limit Lemmas
3.14
and
3.15
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, using the fact that
\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}\sim\sqrt{c}\cdot n^{1/4}=o(n^{3/8})
, this quantity is asymptotic to

\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}}\cdot p\left(x\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}q_{1}\left(y+c\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}}\cdot p\left(x\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}d_{1}(y).

It is then standard (see e.g.
[
Bil68
, Theorem 7.8]
) that this implies that the convergence

\left(\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}-K_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}},\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(W_{1},X_{1})

holds in distribution. This completes the proof.

We are now in position to prove Proposition
3.11
.

Proof 3.18

(Proof of Proposition
3.11
)
.

To simplify notation, set, for
0\leq u\leq 1
,

\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}=\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}-uK_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}},\qquad\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{u}=\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}.

Recall that
\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}\sim{n}/{c}
and
\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}\sim\sqrt{c}\cdot n^{1/4}
.
It is therefore enough to show that conditionally given the event

\mathcal{C}_{n}\coloneqq\{\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=K_{n}+1,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=-1\}
,
the following (stronger) convergence in distribution holds jointly

\left(\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u},\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{u}\right)_{0\leq u\leq 1}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(W^{\mathrm{br}},X^{\mathrm{br}}),

where
W^{\mathrm{br}}
is a Brownian bridge independent of
X^{\mathrm{br}}
. Recall that
d_{u}
denotes the density of
X_{u}
and that
p_{u}
denotes the density of standard Brownian motion at time
u
.

Fix
0<u<1
and
F:\mathbb{D}([0,u],\mathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
a bounded continuous functional. For
\delta\geq 0
, we define
F_{\delta}
by
F_{\delta}(f,g)=F(f,g)\mathbbm{1}_{|f(u)|<\frac{1}{\delta}}
. We fix
\delta>0
and consider the quantity

A^{\delta}_{n}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.F_{\delta}\left(\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{s},\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{s}:0\leq s\leq u\right)\right|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right].

We define the following quantity :

\hskip-8.53581pt\psi\left(\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor},\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}\right)\coloneqq\frac{\phi_{n-K_{n}-\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(K_{n}+1-\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor},-1-\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor})}{\phi_{n-K_{n}}(K_{n}+1,-1)}

(23)

with
\phi_{i}(a,b)=\mathbb{P}\big(\overline{H}^{n}_{i}=a,\overline{B}^{n}_{i}=b\big)
.
By applying the Markov property at time
\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor
, we have

A^{\delta}_{n}=\mathbb{E}\left[F_{\delta}\left(\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{s},\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{s}:0\leq s\leq u\right)\psi\left(\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor},\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}\right)\right].

(24)

We observe that
n-K_{n}-\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor=(1-u)n+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})
(with a deterministic
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})
).
Besides, on the event
\big|\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}\big|<\tfrac{1}{\delta}
,
we have
K_{n}-\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}=(1-u)K_{n}+J_{n}
with
|J_{n}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{1/4})
.
Using Lemma
3.5
, we get that

\phi_{n-K_{n}-\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(K_{n}+1-\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor},-1-\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor})\\
=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{(1-u)n+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})}=K_{n}+1-\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}\right)\\
\cdot\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{(1-u)K_{n}+J_{n}}=(1-u)n+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})-\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor})\right),

(25)

where
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
denotes the conditional probability given
\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n}\rfloor}
and
\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}

(the randomness in Lemma
3.5
comes from
S^{\bullet,n}
and
S^{\circ,n}
).

Conditionally given
\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}
and
\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}
,
Lemma
3.14
gives us

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{(1-u)n+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})}=K_{n}-\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-u}}p\left(-\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}-uK_{n}}{\sqrt{1-u}\cdot\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}}+\eta^{\bullet}_{n}\right)+\frac{\varepsilon^{\bullet}_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}}.

Here,
\eta^{\bullet}_{n}
and
\varepsilon^{\bullet}_{n}
are random sequences
(they depend on
\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}
),
and tend in
L^{\infty}
norm to
0

on the event
\big|\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}\big|<\tfrac{1}{\delta}

(for
\varepsilon^{\bullet}_{n}
, this is because of the uniformity in
k
in Lemma
3.14
).
Since
p
is uniformly continuous
and since
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-u}}p(\tfrac{-x}{\sqrt{1-u}})=p_{1-u}(-x)
,
we have

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{(1-u)n+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})}=K_{n}-\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}}p_{1-u}\left(-\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}-uK_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}}\right)+\frac{\varepsilon^{{}^{\prime}\bullet}_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}},

(26)

for some other random sequence
\varepsilon^{{}^{\prime}\bullet}_{n}
tending to
0
in
L^{\infty}
norm.

Similarly, using Lemma
3.15
and the fact that
q_{1-u}(x+c(1-u))=d_{1-u}(x)
,
on the event
\big|\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}\big|<\tfrac{1}{\delta}
,
we have

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{(1-u)K_{n}+J_{n}}=(1-u)n+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})-\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor})\right)\\
=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}d_{1-u}\left(\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}+\eta^{\circ}_{n}\right)+\frac{\varepsilon^{\circ}_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}},

(27)

conditionally given
\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}
and
\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}
.
Here
\eta^{\circ}_{n}
is a deterministic sequence tending to
0
,
while
\varepsilon^{\circ}_{n}
is random and tends in
L^{\infty}
norm to
0

(because of the uniformity in
j
in Lemma
3.15
).
Since
d_{1-u}
is uniformly continuous, the error
\eta^{\circ}_{n}
in the argument
can be absorbed in the
\varepsilon^{\circ}_{n}
error.

In the proof of Lemma
3.16
, we already saw that

\phi_{n-K_{n}}(K_{n}+1,-1)=\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=K_{n}+1,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=-1\right)\\
\mathop{\sim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}}\cdot p_{1}(0)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}}d_{1}(0).

(28)

By combining Eqs. (
), (
), (
), (
) and (
), we get

\psi\left(\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor},\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}\right)=\frac{p_{1-u}(\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u})}{p_{1}(0)}\frac{d_{1-u}(\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{u})}{d_{1}(0)}+\,\text{error},

(29)

where the (random) error tends to
0
in
L^{\infty}
norm as
n\to\infty
on the event
\big|\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}\big|<\tfrac{1}{\delta}
.
Therefore, using (
), the dominated convergence theorem and Lemma
3.16
, we have :

A^{\delta}_{n}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[F\left(W_{s},X_{s}:0\leq s\leq u\right)\mathbbm{1}_{|W_{u}|<1/\delta}\frac{p_{1-u}(-W_{u})}{p_{1}(0)}\frac{d_{1-u}(-X_{u})}{d_{1}(0)}\right]

(30)

This last quantity is equal to
\mathbb{E}\big[F\big(W^{\textrm{br}}_{s},X^{\textrm{br}}_{s}:0\leq s\leq u\big)\mathbbm{1}_{|W^{\textrm{br}}_{u}|<1/\delta}\big]
by (
).

In remains to take
\delta\rightarrow 0
. Specifically,
taking for
F
the constant functional always equal to
1
in (
), we get

\mathbb{P}\left(|\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}|\geq 1/\delta\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\mathbb{P}\left(|W^{br}_{u}|\geq 1/\delta\right).

(31)

Now fix
\varepsilon>0
and choose
\delta>0
such that
\mathbb{P}\big(|W^{br}_{u}|\geq 1/\delta\big)\leq\varepsilon/\|F\|_{\infty}
. Then, by (
), for every
n
sufficiently large
\mathbb{P}\big(|\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}|\geq 1/\delta\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\big)\leq 2\varepsilon/\|F\|_{\infty}
, so that
\big|A_{n}^{\delta}-A_{n}^{0}\big|\leq 2\varepsilon
. Hence, for every
n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\left|\mathbb{E}\left[F\left(W^{\textrm{br}}_{s},X^{\textrm{br}}_{s}:0\leq s\leq u\right)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\left.F\left(\widehat{H}^{n}_{s},\widehat{B}^{n}_{s}:0\leq s\leq u\right)\right|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt\leq\|F\|_{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(|W^{br}_{u}|\geq 1/\delta\right)+\left|\mathbb{E}\left[F\left(W^{\textrm{br}}_{s},X^{\textrm{br}}_{s}:0\leq s\leq u\right)\mathbbm{1}_{|W^{br}_{u}|<1/\delta}\right]-A_{n}^{\delta}\right|+\left|A_{n}^{\delta}-A_{n}^{0}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84526pt\leq 4\varepsilon.

Hence

\mathbb{E}\left[\left.F\left(\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{s},\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{s}:0\leq s\leq u\right)\right|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right]\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[F\left(W^{\textrm{br}}_{s},X^{\textrm{br}}_{s}:0\leq s\leq u\right)\right].

(32)

A standard time-reversal argument, based on the fact that conditionally given
\mathcal{C}_{n}
, the two paths
(\overline{H}^{n}_{i},\overline{B}^{n}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n-K_{n}}
and
(\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}-\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}-i},\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}-\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}-i})_{1\leq i\leq n-K_{n}}
have the same distribution, shows that the sequence of random variables
(\widehat{H}^{n}_{u},\widehat{B}^{n}_{u})_{0\leq u\leq 1}
is tight in
\mathbb{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The convergence (
) then shows that there is only one possible distributional limit, and this completes the proof.

3.4.
On black vertices with many descendants

We conclude Section
3
by several results, concerning black vertices with many descendants, which build upon the machinery that has just been developed. The first main result (Lemma
3.19
) concerns the modified alternating two-type BGW root which appears in Theorem
3.10
(ii), while the two others (Lemmas
3.21
and
3.25
) will be useful in the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i)
c>0
(convergence to
\mathbf{L}_{c}
).

Throughout Section
3.4
, we assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c>0
as
n\rightarrow\infty

and the offspring distributions
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
are the ones given by Lemma
3.8
.
We use the notation of Theorem
3.10
, namely

—

\mathscr{T}_{n}
is an alternating two-type BGW tree (with black root), with offspring distributions
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
,
conditioned on having
n-K_{n}
black vertices and
K_{n}+1
white vertices ;

—

and
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
is defined as
\mathscr{T}_{n}
,
except that the root has an offspring distribution
\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{\bullet}

given by
\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{\bullet}(i)=\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(i-1)
for
i\geq 1

(the offspring distribution of other vertices and the conditioning are unchanged).

Children of the root.

We start by comparing the number of children of the root in these two models :

Lemma 3.19
.

The following properties are satisfied :

(i)

with probability tending to
1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, the root of
\mathscr{T}_{n}
has only one child ;

(ii)

with probability tending to
1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, the root of
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
has only one child ;

(iii)

we have
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathscr{T}_{n},\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n})\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
d_{\mathrm{TV}}
denotes the total variation distance.

Proof 3.20
.

Denote by
\mathscr{T}^{n}
(resp.
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}
) the unconditioned version of
\mathscr{T}_{n}
(resp.
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
). We keep the superscript
n
to emphasize that the offspring distributions depend on
n
. To simplify notation, for
k\geq 0
, set

P^{n}_{k}=\mathbb{P}\big(\textrm{the root of }\mathscr{T}^{n}\textrm{ has }k\textrm{ children},\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{n}}\rvert=n-K_{n},\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{n}}\rvert=K_{n}+1\big)

and
P^{n}=\mathbb{P}\big(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{n}}\rvert=n-K_{n},\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{n}}\rvert=K_{n}+1\big)
.
Similarly, define
\tilde{P}^{n}_{k}
and
\tilde{P}^{n}
when
\mathscr{T}^{n}
is replaced with
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}
. Clearly,
P^{n}_{0}=\tilde{P}^{n}_{0}=0
.
Finally, set

A_{n,k}=\frac{1}{K_{n}+1}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n-K_{n}-1}=K_{n}+1-k\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{K_{n}+1}=n-K_{n}-1\right).

By decomposing a tree with a black root having
k
children into a forest of
k
trees with white roots and using (
), we have

P^{n}_{k}=k\,\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(k)\cdot A_{n,k}
and
\tilde{P}^{n}_{k}=k\,\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(k-1)\cdot A_{n,k}
.

In the following,
C
will denote a universal constant whose value might change from line to line.
By Lemmas
3.8
,
3.14
and
3.15
, we have the following two asymptotic estimates for
A_{n,k}
:

—

for fixed
k\geq 1
,
A_{n,k}\sim{p(0)\,q_{1}(c)}\cdot\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K_{n}}n^{3/2}}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
;

—

we have
A_{n,k}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{K_{n}}n^{3/2}}
uniformly for all
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
.

When
K_{n}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow 0
, we saw in the proof of Lemma
3.8
that
a_{\bullet}^{n}\rightarrow 1
and that
b_{\bullet}^{n}\sim\frac{K_{n}}{n}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Hence,

\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 1,\qquad\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(1)\quad\mathop{\sim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\frac{K_{n}}{n},

and for
n
large enough,

\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(k)\leq\frac{K_{n}}{{n}}\frac{(k+1)^{k-1}}{k!}\,\textrm{ for }k\geq 1,\quad\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(k)\leq\left(\frac{K_{n}}{{n}}\right)^{2}\frac{(k+1)^{k-1}}{k!}\,\textrm{ for }k\geq 2.

Consequently

P^{n}_{1}\quad\mathop{\sim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad p(0)\,q_{1}(c)\cdot\frac{\sqrt{K_{n}}}{n^{5/2}},\qquad\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}P^{n}_{k}\leq C\frac{K_{n}}{n}\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{K_{n}}}{n^{5/2}}\frac{(k+1)^{k-1}}{k!}=o\big(P^{n}_{1}\big)

since
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow 0
.
Thus
P^{n}\sim P^{n}_{1}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and (i) follows.

Similarly, for (ii), we now have

\tilde{P}^{n}_{1}\ \mathop{\sim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ {p(0)\,q_{1}(c)}\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{K_{n}}n^{3/2}},\qquad\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\tilde{P}^{n}_{k}\leq C\frac{K_{n}}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{K_{n}}n^{3/2}}\frac{(k+1)^{k-1}}{k!}=o\big(\tilde{P}^{n}_{1}\big).

Thus
\tilde{P}^{n}\sim\tilde{P}^{n}_{1}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and (ii) follows.

For (iii), recalling that
\mathbb{A}
denotes the set of all finite plane rooted trees, we show that

\sup_{A\subset\mathbb{A}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}_{n}\in A\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}\in A\right)\right|\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0.

(33)

By (i) and (ii), it is enough to establish this convergence for subsets
A\subset\mathbb{A}
such that the roots of all trees in
A
have
1
child.
If
\tau\in A
, denote by
[\tau]^{1}
the subtree (with white root) grafted on the child of the root of
\tau
.
Denote by
\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}
an (unconditioned) alternating BGW tree with white root (and offspring distributions

\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
). Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}_{n}=\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{P^{n}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{n}=\tau\right)=\frac{1}{P^{n}}\cdot\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(1)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}=[\tau]^{1}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}=\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tilde{P}^{n}}\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}=\tau\right)=\frac{1}{\tilde{P}^{n}}\cdot\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}=[\tau]^{1}\right).

Thus, setting
[A]^{1}=\{[\tau]^{1}:\tau\in A\}
, we get

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}_{n}\in A\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}\in A\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\left|\frac{1}{P^{n}}\cdot\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(1)-\frac{1}{\tilde{P}^{n}}\cdot\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)\right|\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}\in[A]^{1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\left|\frac{1}{P^{n}}\cdot\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(1)-\frac{1}{\tilde{P}^{n}}\cdot\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)\right|\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}}\rvert=n-K_{n}-1,\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}}\rvert=K_{n}+1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\left|\frac{1}{P^{n}}\cdot\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(1)-\frac{1}{\tilde{P}^{n}}\cdot\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)\right|A_{n,1}.

Since
\tfrac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(1)A_{n,1}}{P^{n}}\rightarrow 1
and
\tfrac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)A_{n,1}}{\tilde{P}^{n}}\rightarrow 1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, this completes the proof.

Descendants of black vertices.

We now focus on the model
\mathscr{T}_{n}
and discuss degrees of black vertices.

Lemma 3.21
.

With probability tending to
1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, the tree
\mathscr{T}_{n}
does not contain any black vertex with three or more children.

Proof 3.22
.

Denote by
\Delta^{\bullet}(\mathscr{T}_{n})
the maximum number of children of a black vertex in
\mathscr{T}_{n}
.
The idea is to reformulate the assertion in terms of random walks.
We keep the notation
(H^{n}_{k},B^{n}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
,
\overline{H}^{n}
and
\overline{B}^{n}
which has been introduced in Section
3.2
.
To simplify notation, let us use
\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{n}
for the events

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=K_{n}+1,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=-1\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{+}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=K_{n}+1,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=-1,\overline{B}^{n}_{i}\geq 0\textrm{ for every }1\leq i<n-K_{n}\}.

Since the maximum element of a vector is invariant under cyclic permutations, we may combine Lemmas
3.3
and
3.1
to get that

\displaystyle\Delta^{\bullet}(\mathscr{T}_{n})\

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{(d)}\ \max(H^{n}_{1},\ldots,H^{n}_{n-K_{n}})\textrm{ under }\mathbb{P}\left(\ \cdot\ \big|\mathcal{C}^{+}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{(d)}\ \max(H^{n}_{1},\ldots,H^{n}_{n-K_{n}})\textrm{ under }\mathbb{P}\left(\ \cdot\ \big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right).

Therefore, the lemma asserts that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\max(H^{n}_{1},\ldots,H^{n}_{n-K_{n}})\geq 3\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)=0.

The proof strategy consists in showing the same statement without conditioning
and then using an absolute continuity argument as in the proof of Proposition
3.11
.

The unconditioned statement relies on a simple estimate on the tail of the black vertex offspring distribution
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
.
The proof of Lemma
3.8
shows that we have
|a_{\bullet}^{n}|\leq 2
and
b_{\bullet}^{n}\leq\tfrac{2c}{\sqrt{n}}\leq 1/2
for
n
large enough. Therefore

\mu_{\bullet}^{n}([3,\infty))\leq 2\left(\frac{2c}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{3}\sum_{i\geq 3}\frac{(i+1)^{i-1}}{i!}\,\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i-3}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-3/2}).

(34)

As a consequence,

\mathbb{P}\left(\max(H^{n}_{1},\ldots,H^{n}_{n-K_{n}})\geq 3\right)\leq n\mu_{\bullet}\big([3,\infty)\big)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0.

(35)

In order to use absolute continuity,
we first note that, by cyclic exchangeability,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max(H^{n}_{1},\ldots,H^{n}_{n-K_{n}})\geq 3\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\max(H^{n}_{1},\ldots,H^{n}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor})\geq 3\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}\big(\max(H^{n}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1},\ldots,H^{n}_{n-K_{n}})\geq 3\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}\left(\max(H^{n}_{1},\ldots,H^{n}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor})\geq 3\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right).

To show that this last probability tends to
0
,
we use the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
3.11
.
If
\delta>0
and
G^{\delta}_{n}
is the event
\big\{\big|\tfrac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}-K_{n}/2}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}}\big|<\tfrac{1}{\delta}\big\}
, then by (
),
we have
\mathbb{P}\big(G^{\delta}_{n}|\mathcal{C}_{n}\big)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}\big(|W^{\mathrm{br}}_{1/2}|<1/\delta\big)
.
For fixed
\varepsilon>0
, we can therefore choose
\delta>0
such that
\mathbb{P}\big(G^{\delta}_{n}\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\big)\geq 1-\varepsilon
for
n
sufficiently large. If
\mathcal{E}_{n}
is the event
\{\max(H^{n}_{1},\ldots,H^{n}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor})\geq 3\}
, then

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{n}\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)\leq\varepsilon+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{n}\cap G^{\delta}_{n}\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)\leq\varepsilon+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}\,\mathbbm{1}_{G^{\delta}_{n}}\,\psi\left(\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor},\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\right)\right],

with
\psi
is defined in (
).
But, from (
), there exists a constant
C>0
(which may depend on
\varepsilon
) such that

\mathbbm{1}_{G^{\delta}_{n}}\psi\left(\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor},\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\right)\leq C
for
n
sufficiently large.
On the other hand, from (
),

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\leq\varepsilon/C
for large
n
.
Hence,

\mathbb{P}\left(\max(H_{1},\ldots,H_{n-K_{n}})\geq 3\big|\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)\leq 2\varepsilon+2C\mathbb{P}\left(\max(H_{1},\ldots,H_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor})\geq 3\right)\leq 3\varepsilon.

This completes the proof.

The tree
\mathscr{T}_{n}
may, however, contain black vertices with
2
children.
For such vertices, we will need a further control on their number of descendants.
Before stating and proving such a result, we give a concentration inequality for the lower tails sums of i.i.d. random variables distributed as
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
.

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
u,\delta
be positive constants such that
0<u<1
and
0<\delta<uc
. There exists a constant
A>0
(depending only on
u
,
\delta
and
c
)
such that, for
n
sufficiently large,

\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor}\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\right)\leq e^{-A\sqrt{n}}.

Proof 3.24
.

This is actually a straightforward application of the one-sided version of Hoeffding’s inequality.
Specifically, let
(Y_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be i.i.d. random variable with distribution
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
. Observe that

\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor}\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor}\left(\frac{K_{n}}{n}-Y_{i}\right)\geq\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}-\tfrac{\delta\,\sqrt{n}}{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor}\right).

We then apply
[
Hoe63
, Theorem 3]
, and more precisely
[
Hoe63
, Eq. (2.12)]
(taking, in the notation of
[
Hoe63
]
,
\lfloor u\,n\rfloor
instead of
n
,
X_{i}=\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}-Y_{i}
,
b=\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}
,
t=\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}-\tfrac{\delta\,\sqrt{n}}{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor}
), and obtain

\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor}\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\right)\leq e^{-\tau_{n}h_{n}(\lambda_{n})}

with
h_{n}(\lambda)=\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda}+1\big)\ln(1+\lambda)-1
,
\tau_{n}=\tfrac{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor t}{b}
and
\lambda_{n}=\tfrac{bt}{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}}
. The following asymptotic estimates are readily derived
(recall from Lemma
3.8
that
(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}\sim\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}
) :

t\sim\frac{u\,c-\delta}{u}n^{-1/2},\ \tau_{n}\sim\frac{u\,c-\delta}{c}n,\ \lambda_{n}\sim\frac{uc-\delta}{u}n^{-1/2}\text{ and }h_{n}(\lambda_{n})\sim\frac{\lambda_{n}}{2}.

Thus, for
n
sufficiently large,

\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor}\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{(uc-\delta)^{2}}{3uc}\sqrt{n}}.

This completes the proof.

Lemma 3.25
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. With probability tending to
1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, the tree
\mathscr{T}_{n}
does not contain a black
vertex
v
that has two children, both with at least
\varepsilon\,n
descendants (counting both white and black vertices).

Proof 3.26
.

As before, we consider the alternating two-type
BGW
tree
\mathscr{T}^{n}
(with black root) and offspring distribution

\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
, so that
\mathscr{T}_{n}
is distributed according to
\mathscr{T}^{n}
conditioned on

\{\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{n}}\rvert=n-K_{n},\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{n}}\rvert=K_{n}+1\}
.
If
\tau
is a finite tree and
v\in\bullet_{\tau}
is a black vertex, we denote by

Q_{\varepsilon}(\tau,v)
the property that
v
has two children both with at least
\varepsilon\,|\tau|
descendants. Then

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists v\in\bullet_{\mathscr{T}_{n}}:\,Q_{\varepsilon}(\mathscr{T}_{n},v)\right)\leq\frac{A_{n}}{\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}}\rvert=n-K_{n},\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}}\rvert=K_{n}\right)}

with

A_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau:\lvert\bullet_{\tau}\rvert=n-K_{n},\lvert\circ_{\tau}\rvert=K_{n}+1\\
v\in\bullet_{\tau}\textrm{ with }Q_{\varepsilon}(\tau,v)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{n}=\tau\right).

A couple
(\tau,v)
with
\lvert\bullet_{\tau}\rvert=n-K_{n},\lvert\circ_{\tau}\rvert=K_{n}+1
and
v\in\bullet_{\tau}
with
Q_{\varepsilon}(\tau,v)

can be decomposed into three trees
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{3}
such that :

—

\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are respectively the subtrees (with white root) grafted to the white children of
v
;

—

(\tau_{3},v)
is the tree
obtained from
\tau
by replacing the whole subtree grafted in
v
with a marked leaf.

Figure 9
.
Illustration of the decomposition
(\tau,v)\leftrightarrow(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},(\tau_{3},v))
.

This decomposition defines a bijection (see Figure
9
for an illustration) :

\left\{(\tau,v):Q_{\varepsilon}(\tau),v),\lvert\bullet_{\tau}\rvert=n-K_{n},\lvert\circ_{\tau}\rvert=K_{n}+1\right\}\\
\simeq\left\{(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},(\tau_{3},v)):\begin{array}[]{l}\text{ for $i=1,2$, we have }\lvert\bullet_{\tau_{i}}\rvert+\lvert\circ_{\tau_{i}}\rvert\geq\varepsilon n,\\
\text{ and }\lvert\bullet_{\tau_{1}}\rvert+\lvert\bullet_{\tau_{2}}\rvert+\lvert\bullet_{\tau_{3}}\rvert=n-K_{n},\\
\qquad\ \lvert\circ_{\tau_{1}}\rvert+\lvert\circ_{\tau_{2}}\rvert+\lvert\circ_{\tau_{3}}\rvert=K_{n}+1.\end{array}\right\}.

(36)

In the right-hand side,

\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
have white roots, while
(\tau_{3},v)
has a black root and a marked black leaf.
Moreover,
if
(\tau,v)
is mapped to
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},(\tau_{3},v))
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{n}=\tau\right)=\frac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(2)}{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)}\,\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{1}=\tau_{1}\right)\,\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{2}=\tau_{2}\right)\,\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{3}=\tau_{3}\right),

(37)

where as in Corollary
3.7
,
\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{1}
and
\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{2}
are two-type alternating random BGW trees with offspring distributions
\mu_{\circ}^{n},\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
with white roots, and
\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{3}
is the two-type alternating random BGW tree with black root (which was simply denoted by
\mathscr{T}
before) ; we also take
\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{1}
,
\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{2}
and
\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{3}
to be independent.
The factor
\tfrac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(2)}{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)}
comes from the fact that when we make the decomposition
of
\tau
into three pieces
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},(\tau_{3},v))
, the black vertex
v
with two children
is replaced with a vertex having no children.

Combining (
) and (
), we get

A_{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},(\tau_{3},v)):\\
(\ast)\textrm{ holds}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(2)}{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)}\,\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{1}=\tau_{1}\right)\,\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{2}=\tau_{2}\right)\,\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{3}=\tau_{3}\right),

where
(\ast)
are the conditions appearing in (
).

We now re-index the sum according to the numbers
n^{\circ}_{1}
,
n^{\bullet}_{1}
,
n^{\circ}_{2}
,
n^{\bullet}_{2}

of white and black vertices in
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
, respectively.
We also denote
n^{\circ}_{3}\coloneqq K_{n}+1-n^{\circ}_{1}-n^{\circ}_{2}

and
n^{\bullet}_{3}\coloneqq n-K_{n}-n^{\bullet}_{1}-n^{\bullet}_{2}
, which are the numbers of
white and black vertices in
\tau_{3}
. By bounding from above the number of black leaves of
\tau_{3}
by
\lvert\bullet_{\tau_{3}}\rvert=n^{\bullet}_{3}
, we get that

A_{n}\leq\frac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(2)}{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)}\sum_{n^{\circ}_{1},n^{\bullet}_{1}\atop n^{\circ}_{1}+n^{\bullet}_{1}\geq\varepsilon\,n}\sum_{n^{\circ}_{2},n^{\bullet}_{2}\atop n^{\circ}_{2}+n^{\bullet}_{2}\geq\varepsilon\,n}\Bigg[n^{\bullet}_{3}\,\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{1}}\rvert=n^{\bullet}_{1},\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{1}}\rvert=n^{\circ}_{1}\right)\\
\qquad\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{2}}\rvert=n^{\bullet}_{2},\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}_{2}}\rvert=n^{\circ}_{2}\right)\,\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{3}}\rvert=n^{\bullet}_{3},\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{3}}\rvert=n^{\circ}_{3}\right)\Bigg].

We now use Eqs. (
) and (
)
to express these probabilities using random walks
(recall that
S^{\bullet,n}
and
S^{\circ,n}
are random walks with respective jump distributions given by
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
) :

A_{n}\leq\frac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(2)}{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)}\sum_{n^{\circ}_{1},n^{\bullet}_{1}\atop n^{\circ}_{1}+n^{\bullet}_{1}\geq\varepsilon\,n}\sum_{n^{\circ}_{2},n^{\bullet}_{2}\atop n^{\circ}_{2}+n^{\bullet}_{2}\geq\varepsilon\,n}\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\circ}_{1}}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n^{\bullet}_{1}}=n^{\circ}_{1}-1\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{1}}=n^{\bullet}_{1}\right)\\
\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\circ}_{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n^{\bullet}_{2}}=n^{\circ}_{2}-1\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{2}}=n^{\bullet}_{2}\right)\,\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n^{\bullet}_{3}}=n^{\circ}_{3}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{3}}=n^{\bullet}_{3}-1\right)\Bigg].

Fix a positive constant
\delta\in(0,c\varepsilon)
.
We split the double sum in the right-hand side in two parts
\varSigma_{1}
and
\varSigma_{2}
:

\varSigma_{1}
is the sum of terms for which both
n^{\circ}_{1}\geq\delta\sqrt{n}
and

n^{\circ}_{2}\geq\delta\sqrt{n}
,
while
\varSigma_{2}
contains all other terms.

Bounding
\varSigma_{1}
.

Write

\varSigma_{1}\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}n}\sum_{n^{\circ}_{1},n^{\bullet}_{1}\atop n^{\circ}_{1}+n^{\bullet}_{1}\geq\varepsilon\,n}\sum_{n^{\circ}_{2},n^{\bullet}_{2}\atop n^{\circ}_{2}+n^{\bullet}_{2}\geq\varepsilon\,n}\Bigg[\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n^{\bullet}_{1}}=n^{\circ}_{1}-1\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{1}}=n^{\bullet}_{1}\right)\\
\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n^{\bullet}_{2}}=n^{\circ}_{2}-1\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{2}}=n^{\bullet}_{2}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n^{\bullet}_{3}}=n^{\circ}_{3}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{3}}=n^{\bullet}_{3}-1\right)\Bigg].

Since
n^{\circ}_{1}\leq K_{n}+1\sim c\sqrt{n}
, the condition
n^{\circ}_{1}+n^{\bullet}_{1}\geq\varepsilon\,n

implies that we can find
u\in(\tfrac{\delta}{c},\varepsilon)
such that
n^{\bullet}_{1}\geq u\,n
for
n
large enough.
Similarly, we have
n^{\bullet}_{2}\geq u\,n
for
n
large enough.
Then Lemma
3.14
implies that

\mathbb{P}\big(S^{\bullet,n}_{n^{\bullet}_{1}}=n^{\circ}_{1}-1\big)\leq C_{1}n^{-1/4}

for
n
large enough and for some constant
C_{1}>0
,
uniformly for
n^{\bullet}_{1}\in\{\lceil un\rceil,\dots,n\}
and for
n^{\circ}_{1}\geq 1
.
The same obviously holds when replacing indices
1
with
2
.
By using the trivial bound
\mathbb{P}\big(S^{\bullet,n}_{n^{\bullet}_{3}}=n^{\circ}_{3}\big)\leq 1
, we get

\varSigma_{1}\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}n}\,C_{1}^{2}n^{-1/2}\sum_{n^{\circ}_{1},n^{\circ}_{2}}\left[\sum_{n^{\bullet}_{1},n^{\bullet}_{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{1}}=n^{\bullet}_{1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{2}}=n^{\bullet}_{2}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{3}}=n^{\bullet}_{3}-1\right)\right].

We claim that

\sum_{n^{\bullet}_{1},n^{\bullet}_{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{1}}=n^{\bullet}_{1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{2}}=n^{\bullet}_{2}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{3}}=n^{\bullet}_{3}-1\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{K_{n}+1}=n-K_{n}-1\right).

To see this, note that
n^{\circ}_{1}+n^{\circ}_{2}+n^{\circ}_{3}=K_{n}+1
and
n^{\bullet}_{1}+n^{\bullet}_{2}+n^{\bullet}_{3}-1=n-K_{n}-1
,
and simply split the event
\{S^{\circ,n}_{K_{n}+1}=n-K_{n}-1\}
,
according to the intermediate values
S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{1}}
and
S^{\circ,n}_{n^{\circ}_{1}+n^{\circ}_{2}}
.

By Lemma
3.15
, there exists a constant
C_{2}>0
such that
\mathbb{P}\big(S^{\circ,n}_{K_{n}+1}=n-K_{n}-1\big)\leq C_{2}/n
for
n
sufficiently large. Also, the number of possible values for
n^{\circ}_{1}
is at most
K_{n}+1
; and similarly for
n^{\circ}_{2}
.
A straightforward analogue of (
) gives
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(2)=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
,
while
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)
tends to a constant.
Bringing everything together, there exists a constant
C_{3}>0
such that

\frac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(2)}{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)}\varSigma_{1}\leq\frac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(2)}{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(0)}\,\frac{1}{\delta^{2}n}\,C_{1}^{2}n^{-1/2}\sum_{n^{\circ}_{1},n^{\circ}_{2}}\frac{C_{2}}{n}\leq C_{3}\,\frac{1}{n}\,\frac{1}{\delta^{2}n}n^{-1/2}\,n\,\frac{1}{n}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-5/2}).

Bounding
\varSigma_{2}
.

As noticed above, we can find
u\in(\tfrac{\delta}{c},\varepsilon)
such that for
n
sufficiently large, in all summands, we have
n_{1}^{\bullet}\geq u\,n
and
n_{2}^{\bullet}\geq u\,n
.
Therefore, if
n_{1}^{\circ}<\delta\,\sqrt{n}
, Lemma
3.23
implies

\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n^{\bullet}_{1}}=n^{\circ}_{1}-1\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{\lfloor u\,n\rfloor}\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\right)\leq\exp(-A\sqrt{n}).

A similar bound holds if
n_{2}^{\circ}<\delta\,\sqrt{n}
.
All the summands in
\varSigma_{2}
are therefore bounded by
\exp(-A\sqrt{n})
.
Since the number of terms is polynomial, we get that

\varSigma_{2}
is exponentially small.

To conclude,
A_{n}
is
\mathcal{O}(n^{-5/2})
.
Using (
) and the local limit theorems (Lemmas
3.14
and
3.15
),
we know that
\mathbb{P}\big(\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{n}}\rvert=n-K_{n},\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{n}}\rvert=K_{n}+1\big)\sim\ Cn^{-9/4}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, for a certain constant
C>0
.
We have therefore proved that

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists v\in\bullet_{\mathscr{T}_{n}}:\,Q_{\varepsilon}(\mathscr{T}_{n},v)\right)=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/4}),

which tends to
0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. This completes the proof.

3.5.
Local limit theorems

We now establish two local limit estimates concerning the random walks
S^{\bullet,n}
and
S^{\circ,n}

(Lemmas
3.14
and
3.15
).

Local limit theorem for
S^{\bullet,n}
.

Let
\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)=\mathbb{E}\big[e^{itS^{\bullet,n}_{1}}\big]=\tfrac{F(b_{\bullet}^{n}e^{it})}{F(b_{\bullet}^{n})}
be the characteristic function of
S^{\bullet,n}_{1}
,
i.e. of the distribution
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
.
(Recall that
F(z)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\tfrac{(k+1)^{k-1}}{k!}z^{k}
was introduced in the proof of Lemma
3.8
.)

Lemma 3.27
.

Assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow 0
and
K_{n}\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. For every
n
sufficiently large, for every
0\leq|t|\leq\pi
we have

\ln|\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)|\leq-\frac{K_{n}}{8n}t^{2}.

(38)

Proof 3.28
.

We take two different approaches depending on the distance of
|t|
to
0
.

First assume that
|t|\leq 1
. We have (see e.g.
[
Dur10
, Lemma 3.3.7]
)

\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)=1+\textrm{i}\mathbb{E}[S^{\bullet,n}_{1}]t-\mathbb{E}[(S^{\bullet,n}_{1})^{2}]\frac{t^{2}}{2}+r^{n}_{1}(t),

with
|r^{n}_{1}(t)|\leq|t|^{3}\mathbb{E}[(S^{\bullet,n}_{1})^{3}]/6
for every
n\geq 1
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. By using the expansion of
F
around
0
, we see that
\mathbb{E}[S^{\bullet,n}_{1}]\sim\mathbb{E}[(S^{\bullet,n}_{1})^{2}]\sim\mathbb{E}[(S^{\bullet,n}_{1})^{3}]\sim\frac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, so that

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt|\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\textrm{i}\mathbb{E}[S^{\bullet,n}_{1}]t-\mathbb{E}[(S^{\bullet,n}_{1})^{2}]\frac{t^{2}}{2}+r^{n}_{1}(t)\right)\left(1-\textrm{i}\mathbb{E}[S^{\bullet,n}_{1}]t-\mathbb{E}[(S^{\bullet,n}_{1})^{2}]\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\overline{r^{n}_{1}(t)}\right)

\displaystyle=1-(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}t^{2}+r^{n}_{2}(t),

where
|r^{n}_{2}(t)|\leq|t|^{3}K_{n}/(2n)
for every
n
sufficiently large, uniformly for
t\in[-1,1]
.
Therefore

2\ln|\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)|\leq-(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}t^{2}+|t|^{3}K_{n}/(2n)=-\frac{K_{n}}{n}t^{2}\left(\frac{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}{K_{n}}-|t|/2\right)

for every
n
sufficiently large, uniformly for
t\in[-1,1]
.
Since
(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}\sim K_{n}/n
as
n\rightarrow\infty
by Lemma
3.8
(ii), it follows that for every
n
sufficiently large and
0\leq|t|\leq 1
we have
\ln|\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)|\leq-\frac{K_{n}}{8n}t^{2}
.

Now assume that
1\leq|t|\leq\pi
. Since
F(z)=1+z+o(z)
as
z\rightarrow 0
and, by definition,
\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)=\tfrac{F(b_{\bullet}^{n}e^{it})}{F(b_{\bullet}^{n})}
,
we have

|\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)|^{2}=(1+b_{\bullet}^{n}(e^{it}-1)+o(b_{\bullet}^{n}))(1+b_{\bullet}^{n}(e^{-it}-1)+o(b_{\bullet}^{n}))=1+2b_{\bullet}^{n}(\cos(t)-1)+o(b_{\bullet}^{n}),

where the
o(b_{\bullet}^{n})
is uniform in
1\leq|t|\leq\pi
.
Therefore, for every
1\leq|t|\leq\pi
, for every
n
sufficiently large,

2\ln|\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)|\leq-b_{\bullet}^{n}\frac{t^{2}}{3}+o(b_{\bullet}^{n})\leq-\frac{K_{n}}{4n}t^{2},

where for the last inequality we have used the fact that
b_{\bullet}^{n}\sim\frac{K_{n}}{n}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
by Lemma
3.8
(ii) and that
t
is not too close to
0
.

Proof 3.29

(Proof of Lemma
3.14
)
.

We first check that the convergence

\mathbb{E}\bigg[e^{it\frac{S^{\bullet,n}_{N}-N\frac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}}}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}}\bigg]\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}

(39)

holds uniformly for
un\leq N\leq n
and
t
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
. As in the proof of Lemma
3.27
, since
\tfrac{\mathbb{E}[(S^{\bullet,n}_{1})^{3}]}{(D^{\bullet}_{n,N})^{3}}=\mathcal{O}(\tfrac{1}{n\sqrt{K_{n}}})=o(\frac{1}{n})
, we have

\phi^{\bullet}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}\right)=1+\textrm{i}\mathbb{E}[S^{\bullet,n}_{1}]\frac{t}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}-\mathbb{E}[(S^{\bullet,n}_{1})^{2}]\frac{t^{2}}{2(D^{\bullet}_{n,N})^{2}}+o\left(\frac{1}{n}\right),

where the
o
is uniform when
un\leq N\leq n
and
t
belongs to a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}
, so that

\ln\phi^{\bullet}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}\right)=it\frac{K_{n}+1}{(n-K_{n})D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}}{(D^{\bullet}_{n,N})^{2}}+o\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

Thus

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{it\frac{S^{\bullet,n}_{N}-N\frac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}}}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}}\right]=\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{N(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}}{(D^{\bullet}_{n,N})^{2}}+o\left(\frac{N}{n}\right)\right).

Since
(D^{\bullet}_{n,N})^{2}=N(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}
, this implies (
).

We then follow the steps of the analytic proof of the standard local limit theorem for a sequence of independent identically distributed random variables. The main difficulty is that the distribution of
S^{\bullet,n}_{1}
depends on
n
. To simplify notation, set
f(t)=e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. By Fourier inversion we have, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

p(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-itx}f(t){{\rm d}}t.

(40)

Also, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{N}=k\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-itk}\phi^{\bullet}_{n}(t)^{N}{\mathrm{d}}t.

In the following, we only consider values of
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that
N\frac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}}+xD^{\bullet}_{n,N}
is an integer.
For such
x
,

D^{\bullet}_{n,N}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{N}=N\frac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}}+xD^{\bullet}_{n,N}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int^{\pi D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}_{-\pi D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}e^{-itx}\left(\phi^{\bullet}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}\right)e^{-it\frac{K_{n}+1}{(n-K_{n})D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}}\right)^{N}{\mathrm{d}}t.

Therefore for every fixed
A>0
, for
n
sufficiently large,

\left|D^{\bullet}_{n,N}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{N}=N\frac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}}+xD^{\bullet}_{n,N}\right)-p(x)\right|\\
\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\big(|I^{(1)}_{A}(x,N,n)|+|I^{(2)}_{A}(x,N,n)|+|I^{(3)}(x)|\big),

where

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{A}(x,N,n)

\displaystyle=\int_{-A}^{A}e^{-itx}\left(\left(\phi^{\bullet}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}\right)e^{-it\frac{K_{n}+1}{(n-K_{n})D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}}\right)^{N}-f(t)\right){\mathrm{d}}t,

\displaystyle I^{(2)}_{A}(x,N,n)

\displaystyle=\int_{A<|t|<\pi D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}e^{-itx-it\frac{(K_{n}+1)N}{(n-K_{n})D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}}\phi^{\bullet}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}\right)^{N}{\mathrm{d}}t,

\displaystyle I^{(3)}_{A}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{|t|>A}e^{-itx}f(t){\mathrm{d}}t.

We shall check that for every fixed
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
A>0
such that for every
n
sufficiently large, for every
un\leq N\leq n
, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
(with the above integrality condition),
|I^{(1)}_{A}(x,N,n)|\leq\varepsilon
,
|I^{(2)}_{A}(x,N,n)|\leq\varepsilon
and
|I^{(3)}_{A}(x)|\leq\varepsilon
.

We choose
A>0
such that

2\int_{A}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{1}{9}t^{2}}{{\rm d}}t<\varepsilon.

(41)

Bounding
|I^{(1)}_{A}(x,N,n)|
.
Since the convergence (
) holds uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
, for
n
sufficiently large, for every
un\leq N\leq n
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
|I^{(1)}_{A}(x,N,n)|\leq\varepsilon
.

Bounding
|I^{(3)}_{A}(x)|
.
By the choice of
A
in (
)
since
|f(t)|=e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{9}t^{2}}
,
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have
|I^{(3)}_{A}(x)|\leq\varepsilon
.

Bounding
|I^{(2)}_{{A}}(x,N,n)|
.
By Lemma
3.27
, for every
n
sufficiently large, for every
un\leq N\leq n
,
A<|t|\leq\pi D^{\bullet}_{n,N}
we have

|I^{(2)}(x,N,n)|\leq 2\int_{A}^{\pi D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}e^{-\frac{K_{n}N}{8n(D^{\bullet}_{n,N})^{2}}\cdot t^{2}}{{\rm d}}t\leq 2\int_{A}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{1}{9}t^{2}}{{\rm d}}t,

which is less than
\varepsilon
by (
). This completes the proof.

Local limit theorem for
S^{\circ,n}
.

In the case of
S^{\circ,n}
, the analysis is more subtle.
As above, we start with an estimate on the characteristic function
of the step distribution of the random walk

\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[e^{itS^{\circ,n}_{1}}\right]=\frac{F(b_{\circ}^{n}e^{it})}{F(b_{\circ}^{n})}.

To simplify notation, set
y_{n}=1/e-b_{\circ}^{n}
.

Lemma 3.30
.

Assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c>0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. There exist constants
A_{0},\varepsilon,\kappa>0
, which may only depend on
c
, such that the following holds.
For every
n
sufficiently large and for every
|t|\in(\frac{A_{0}}{D^{\circ}_{n}},\varepsilon)
, we have
|\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)|\leq e^{-\kappa|t|^{1/2}}
.

Proof 3.31
.

We start with some preliminary observations which fix the values of the different constants.

–

Since
D^{\circ}_{n}\sim n/c
and
y_{n}\sim\frac{c^{2}}{2en}
(as was seen in the proof of Lemma
3.8
),
we can find a constant
\gamma>0
depending only on
c
such that
the conditions
t\geq{A_{0}}/{D^{\circ}_{n}}
and
A_{0}\geq 1
imply

t\geq{\gamma A_{0}}/{n}
and
t\geq\gamma y_{n}
,
for
n
sufficiently large.

–

We then fix
A_{0}\geq 1
such that
\frac{2c}{\sqrt{\gamma A_{0}}}\leq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{e}}
.

–

From the expansion
\ln F\big(\tfrac{1}{e}-z\big)=1-\sqrt{2e}\sqrt{z}+o(\sqrt{z})
, there exists
\eta>0
such that

\operatorname{Re}(z)\geq 0,\,|z|\leq\eta\qquad\implies\qquad\operatorname{Re}\ln F\left(\frac{1}{e}-z\right)\leq 1-\operatorname{Re}\sqrt{z}.

(42)

Then set
\varepsilon=\sqrt{\eta^{2}/(e^{-2}+\gamma^{-2})}
.

We now turn to the main part of the proof. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, since

\ln|\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)|=\operatorname{Re}\ln\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)
, we have

\ln|\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)|=\operatorname{Re}\ln F(b_{\circ}^{n}e^{it})-\ln F(b_{\circ}^{n}).

(43)

We first study the term
\ln F(b_{\circ}^{n})
.
By (
) and (
), we have
\ln F(b_{\circ}^{n})=1-\tfrac{c}{\sqrt{n}}+o\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big)
. As a consequence, for every
n
sufficiently large, for every
{A_{0}}/{D^{\circ}_{n}}\leq|t|\leq 1
, we have

\ln F(b_{\circ}^{n})\geq 1-\frac{2c}{\sqrt{n}}\geq 1-\frac{2c}{\sqrt{\gamma A_{0}}}\sqrt{|t|}.

(44)

Consider now the term
\operatorname{Re}\ln F(b_{\circ}^{n}e^{it})
.
To this end, we set
z=z_{n}(t)=\tfrac{1}{e}-b_{\circ}^{n}e^{it}
.
Note that we always have
\operatorname{Re}(z_{n}(t))\geq 0
.
Observing that
e^{-it}z_{n}(t)=\tfrac{1}{e}(e^{-it}-1)+y_{n}
, we have

|z_{n}(t)|^{2}=\tfrac{1}{e^{2}}\big|e^{-it}-1\big|^{2}+\tfrac{2}{e}\big\langle y_{n},e^{-it}-1\big\rangle+|y_{n}|^{2}=\frac{2(1-\cos(t))}{e^{2}}-\frac{2y_{n}(1-\cos(t))}{e}+y_{n}^{2}.

(45)

For
n
sufficiently large and
|t|\leq\varepsilon
, it follows that

|z_{n}(t)|^{2}\leq\left(\frac{1}{e^{2}}+\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\right)t^{2}\leq\eta^{2}.

(46)

Using (
), we have

\operatorname{Re}\ln F(b_{\circ}^{n}e^{it})\leq 1-\operatorname{Re}\sqrt{z_{n}(t)}.

Since
\operatorname{Re}z_{n}(t)\geq 0
, we have

\operatorname{Re}\sqrt{z_{n}(t)}=\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{Re}(z_{n}(t))+|z_{n}(t)|}{2}}\geq\sqrt{\frac{|z_{n}(t)|}{2}}.

But, by (
), for
n
sufficiently large and for every
|t|
in
(A_{0}/D^{\circ}_{n},1)
,

|z_{n}(t)|^{2}=\frac{2(1-\cos(t))}{e^{2}}(1-y_{n}e)+y_{n}^{2}\geq\frac{1-\cos(t)}{e^{2}}\geq\frac{t^{2}}{4e^{2}}.

As a consequence
\operatorname{Re}\sqrt{z_{n}(t)}\geq\frac{\sqrt{|t|}}{2\sqrt{e}}
, so that

\operatorname{Re}\ln F(b_{\circ}^{n}e^{it})\leq 1-\frac{\sqrt{|t|}}{2\sqrt{e}}.

(47)

To conclude, by combining (
) with (
) and (
),
we get that for every
n
sufficiently large, for every
|t|
in
(A_{0}/D^{\circ}_{n},\varepsilon)
,

\ln|\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)|\leq\left(\frac{2c}{\sqrt{\gamma A_{0}}}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{e}}\right)\cdot\sqrt{|t|}\leq-\frac{1}{4\sqrt{e}}\sqrt{|t|}.

This completes the proof, with
\kappa=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{e}}
.

We are now ready to tackle the proof of Lemma
3.15
.

Proof 3.32

(Proof of Lemma
3.15
)
.

The first step is to show the convergence

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{it\frac{S^{\circ,n}_{uK_{n}+j}}{D^{\circ}_{n}}}\right]\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\exp\left(uc^{2}\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{2it}{c}}\right)\right),

(48)

uniformly for
t
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
and
|j|\leq n^{3/8}
.

The characteristic function
\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{itS^{\circ,n}_{1}}\right]
of
S^{\circ,n}_{1}

is given as
\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)=\frac{F(b_{\circ}^{n}e^{it})}{F(b_{\circ}^{n})}
,
where
b_{\circ}^{n}
has been chosen such that
\frac{b_{\circ}^{n}F^{\prime}(b_{\circ}^{n})}{F(b_{\circ}^{n})}=\frac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}
.
Recall that
b_{\circ}^{n}=1/e-y_{n}
with

y_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2e}\big(\frac{K_{n}}{n}\big)^{2}\sim\frac{c^{2}}{2e}\cdot\frac{1}{n}

(from (
)).
Since
D^{\circ}_{n}\sim n/c
, we have, uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

b_{\circ}^{n}e^{\frac{it}{D^{\circ}_{n}}}=(1/e-y_{n})e^{\frac{it}{D^{\circ}_{n}}}=1/e-\left(\frac{c^{2}}{2e}-\frac{itc}{e}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{{n}}+o\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

By (
), we have
\ln F\left(\frac{1}{e}-z\right)=1-\sqrt{2e}\sqrt{z}+o(\sqrt{z})
as
z\rightarrow 0
.
As a consequence as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\ln\phi^{\circ}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)=-\sqrt{2e}\sqrt{\frac{c^{2}}{2e}-\frac{itc}{e}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{2e}\sqrt{\frac{c^{2}}{2e}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
. Therefore

(uK_{n}+j)\ln\phi^{\circ}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad uc^{2}-uc\sqrt{c^{2}-2itc},

uniformly when
t
belongs to compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
and
|j|\leq n^{3/8}
, which implies (
).

As in the proof of Lemma
3.14
, the second step consists in estimating
\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{uK_{n}+j}=k\right)
by Fourier inversion.
We first let, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

f(t)=\exp\left(uc^{2}\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{2it}{c}}\right)\right)

be the expression appearing in (
).
By
[
Kyp06
, Sec.1.2.5]
),
f
is the characteristic function of a random variable with explicit density

q_{u}(x)=\left(\frac{u^{2}c^{3}}{2\pi x^{3}}\right)^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{c(x-uc)^{2}}{2x}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{x>0},

so that by Fourier inversion we have, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

q_{u}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}t}e^{-itx}\exp\left(uc^{2}\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{2it}{c}}\right)\right).

(49)

On the other hand,
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{uK_{n}+j}=k\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-itk}\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)^{uK_{n}+j}{\mathrm{d}}t.

Therefore, assuming that
x\in\mathbb{R}
is chosen so that
xD^{\circ}_{n}
is an integer, we get that

D^{\circ}_{n}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{uK_{n}+j}=xD^{\circ}_{n}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int^{\pi D^{\circ}_{n}}_{-\pi D^{\circ}_{n}}e^{-itx}\phi^{\circ}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)^{uK_{n}+j}{\mathrm{d}}t.

We deduce that for every fixed
A>0
and
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
, for
n
sufficiently large, it holds

\left|D^{\circ}_{n}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{uK_{n}+j}=xD^{\circ}_{n}\right)-q_{u}(x)\right|\\
\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}(|I^{(1)}_{A}(x,j,n)|+|I^{(2)}_{\varepsilon,A}(x,j,n)|+|I^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(x,j,n)|+|I^{(4)}_{A}(x)|),

where

I^{(1)}_{A}(x,j,n)=\int_{-A}^{A}e^{-itx}\left(\phi^{\circ}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)^{uK_{n}+j}-f(t)\right){\mathrm{d}}t

I^{(2)}_{\varepsilon,A}(x,j,n)=\int_{A<|t|<\varepsilon D^{\circ}_{n}}e^{-itx}\phi^{\circ}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)^{uK_{n}+j}{\mathrm{d}}t,

I^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(x,j,n)=\int_{\varepsilon D^{\circ}_{n}<|t|<\pi D^{\circ}_{n}}e^{-itx}\phi^{\circ}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)^{uK_{n}+j}{\mathrm{d}}t,\qquad I^{(4)}_{A}(x)=\int_{|t|>A}e^{-itx}f(t){\mathrm{d}}t.

We shall check that for every fixed
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, there exists
A>0
and
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
such that for every
n
sufficiently large, for every
|j|\leq n^{3/8}
, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

|I^{(1)}_{A}(x,j,n)|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime},|I^{(2)}_{\varepsilon,A}(x,j,n)|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime},|I^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(x,j,n)|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime},|I^{(4)}_{A}(x)|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

Fix
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
. We first explain how to choose
A
and
\varepsilon
.
As a preliminary observation, note that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0

(which only depend on
u
and
c
) such that
|f(t)|\leq C_{1}e^{-C_{1}^{-1}\sqrt{t}}
for
t\geq 0

(this is straightforward, using the explicit expression of
f
).
Let
A_{0}
and
\kappa
be the constants given by Lemma
3.30
.
We may choose
A\geq A_{0}
such that

2\int_{A}^{\infty}|f(t)|\mathrm{d}t<\varepsilon^{\prime}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad 2\int_{A}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\kappa uc^{3/2}}{2}t^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}t<\varepsilon^{\prime}.

(50)

We then let
\varepsilon
be also given by Lemma
3.30
.

Bounding
|I^{(1)}_{A}(x,j,n)|
.
Since the convergence (
) holds uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
and
|j|\leq n^{3/8}
, for
n
sufficiently large, for every
|j|\leq n^{3/8}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
|I^{(1)}_{A}(x,j,n)|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

Bounding
|I^{(4)}_{A}(x)|
.
By the choice of
A
in (
), for every
n
sufficiently large, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have
|I^{(4)}_{A}(x,n)|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

Bounding
|I^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(x,j,n)|
.
Set
\phi(t)=\frac{F(e^{it}/e)}{F(1/e)}
, which is the characteristic function of the probability distribution
\frac{(i+1)^{(i-1)}}{e^{i+1}i!}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Since the latter is non lattice, we have
|\phi(t)|<1
for
t\in(0,2\pi)
(see e.g.
[
Dur10
, Theorem 3.5.1]
).
Therefore, there exists
C_{2}>0
such that
|\phi(t)|<e^{-2C_{2}}
for
\varepsilon\leq|t|\leq\pi
.
Since
F
is a power series with nonnegative coefficients and converges in
1/e
,
it converges uniformly on
\{z:|z|\leq 1/e\}
and is continuous on this compact set.
It is therefore uniformly continuous and
we have
\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)\rightarrow\phi(t)
, uniformly for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
In particular, for
n
sufficiently large and
t
with
\varepsilon\leq|t|\leq\pi
,
we have
|\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)|<e^{-C_{2}}
.
Therefore, for every
n
sufficiently large, for every
|j|\leq n^{3/8}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|I^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(x,j,n)\right|\leq 2\int_{\varepsilon D^{\circ}_{n}}^{\pi D^{\circ}_{n}}e^{-C_{2}(uK_{n}+n^{3/8})}\mathrm{d}t\leq 2\pi D^{\circ}_{n}\cdot(e^{-C_{2}uc\sqrt{n}/2})\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}.

Bounding
|I^{(2)}_{\varepsilon,{A}}(x,j,n)|
.
By Lemma
3.30
, for every
A<|t|\leq\varepsilon D^{\circ}_{n}
we have

\left|\phi^{\circ}_{n}\left(\frac{t}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)\right|\leq e^{-\kappa\left(\frac{|t|}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)^{1/2}}.

Hence, for
n
sufficiently large, for every
|j|\leq n^{3/8}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|I^{(2)}_{\varepsilon,A}(x,j,n)\right|\leq 2\int_{A}^{\varepsilon D^{\circ}_{n}}\left(e^{-\kappa\left(\frac{t}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)^{1/2}}\right)^{uK_{n}-n^{3/8}}\mathrm{d}t.

Since
K_{n}\sim c\sqrt{n}
and
D^{\circ}_{n}\sim n/c
, it follows that for
n
sufficiently large, for every
|j|\leq n^{3/8}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|I^{(2)}_{\varepsilon,A}(x,j,n)\right|\leq 2\int_{A}^{\varepsilon D^{\circ}_{n}}e^{-\frac{\kappa uc^{3/2}}{2}t^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}t\leq 2\int_{A}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\kappa uc^{3/2}}{2}t^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}t,

which is less than
\varepsilon^{\prime}
by (
). This completes the proof.

4.
Minimal factorizations and laminations

Table 3.
Table of the main notation and symbols appearing in Section
4
.

\mathbf{L}_{\infty}

The Brownian triangulation.

\mathbf{L}_{c}

The lamination coded by an excursion of a Lévy process with characteristic exponent given by (
).

(t_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,t_{n-1}^{(n)})

A minimal factorization of
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
.

\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}

The non-crossing partition
\mathcal{P}(t_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,t_{K_{n}}^{(n)})
and its associated compact set.

\mathcal{F}_{n}
,
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}

The non-crossing forest
\mathcal{F}(t_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,t_{K_{n}}^{(n)})
and its associated compact set.

T_{n}

The tree
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
coding the non-crossing partition
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

A
geodesic lamination

L
of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
is a closed subset of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
such that
L
can be written as the union non-crossing chords, i.e. which do not intersect in
\mathbb{D}
.
Recall that by convention,
\{x\}
is a chord for every
x\in\mathbb{S}
. In particular,
\mathbb{S}
is a geodesic lamination. In the sequel, by lamination we will always mean geodesic lamination of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
. It is well known (and simple to see) that the set of all geodesic laminations of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
equipped with Hausdorff topology is compact.

4.1.
Definition of the random lamination
\mathbf{L}_{c}

We start with defining
\mathbf{L}_{c}
in the three cases
c=0
,
c=\infty
and
c\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. First, for
c=0
, we simply let
\mathbf{L}_{0}
be the unit circle
\mathbb{S}
.

Case
c=\infty
.

Let
Z=(Z_{u})_{0\leq u\leq 1}\in\mathbb{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
be a continuous function.
We assume
Z_{0}=Z_{1}=0
, as well as the following property :

(C1)

Local minima of
Z
have distinct values,
meaning that for every
0\leq s<t\leq 1
, there exists at most one value
r\in(s,t)
such that
Z_{r}=\inf_{[s,t]}Z
.

Following
[
Ald94a
,
LGP08
]
, we construct a lamination
L(Z)
from
Z
as follows.
For
every
s,t\in[0,1]
, we set
s\,{\sim}^{Z}\,t
if we have
Z_{s}=Z_{t}=\min_{[s\wedge t,s\vee t]}Z
. We set

L(Z)\quad\coloneqq\quad\bigcup_{s\sim^{Z}t}\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t}\big].

(51)

Then
L(Z)
is a geodesic lamination, which is a triangulation (in the sense that the
complement of
L(Z)
in
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
is a
disjoint union of open Euclidean triangles whose vertices belong to
the unit circle). In particular,
L(Z)
is maximal with respect to inclusion for geodesic laminations (see
[
LGP08
, Proposition 2.1]
). Observe that
\mathbb{S}\subset L(Z)
.
Using a continuity argument and the fact that the set of local minima is countable, it is a simple matter to see that

L(Z)=\overline{\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim^{Z}t,s\neq t\\
s,t\textrm{ are not local minima}\end{subarray}}\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t}\big]}.

(52)

(Roughly speaking, this means that
L(Z)
is the closure of all non-trivial chords which do not belong to triangles.)
In a similar way, since
Z
is continuous, we have

s\sim^{Z}t\ \implies\ \forall\varepsilon>0,\,\exists s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\textrm{ such that }s^{\prime}\sim^{Z}t^{\prime}\textrm{ with }0<|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon,0<|t-t^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon.

(53)

(Roughly speaking, this means that chords are not isolated).

We now consider the
Brownian excursion

\mathbbm{e}
,
which is informally defined as a Brownian motion
conditioned to return to the origin at time
1
and to stay positive on the time interval
(0,1)
.
It is well known that it satisfies
(C1)

a.s. We then define

\mathbf{L}_{\infty}\coloneqq L(\mathbbm{e})

to be the Brownian triangulation, see Figure
10
for a simulation.

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[scale]{Excursion_Brownienne.pdf}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[scale]{Triangulation_Brownienne.pdf}\end{array}

Figure 10.
A realization of the Brownian excursion
\mathbbm{e}
and its associated lamination.
The latter is therefore a realization of the Brownian triangulation
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
.

The Brownian triangulation has been introduced by Aldous
[
Ald94a
,
Ald94b
]
, and it has been shown to be the universal limit of many different families of random non-crossing configurations
[
Kor14
,
CK14
,
KM17
]
.

Case
c>0
.

Let
Z\in{\mathbb{D}}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
be a càdlàg function.
For
t\in[0,1]
, we denote
Z_{t-}
the left-hand limit of
Z
in
t
,
and set
\Delta Z_{t}=Z_{t}-Z_{t-}
, with the convention
Z_{0-}=0
.
We fix a function
Z\in{\mathbb{D}}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
such that
Z_{1}=0
,
Z_{t}>0
and
\Delta Z_{t}\geq 0
for every
t\in(0,1)
,
and we define the following four properties :

(H0)

\{t\in(0,1):\Delta Z_{t}>0\}
is dense in
[0,1]
.

(H1)

For every
0\leq s<t\leq 1
, there exists at most one value
r\in(s,t)
such that
Z_{r}=\inf_{[s,t]}Z
.

(H2)

For every
t\in[0,1)
such that
\Delta Z_{t}>0
, we have
\inf_{[t,t+\varepsilon]}Z<Z_{t}
for every
0<\varepsilon\leq 1-t
;

(H3)

For every
t\in(0,1)
such that
Z
attains a local minimum at
t-
(meaning that there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
Z_{t-}=\inf_{[t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon]}Z
), if
s=\sup\{u\in[0,t]:Z_{u}<Z_{t-}\}
, then
\Delta Z_{s}>0
and
Z_{s-}<Z_{t-}<Z_{s}
.

Following
[
Kor14
]
,
we construct a lamination
L(Z)
from a function
Z
with the above 4 properties
(note that in contrast with
[
Kor14
]
, we have one less property,
and the analogue of
(H2)
differs since we accept here the case
Z_{0}>0
).
To this end, we define a relation (which is not an equivalence relation in general) on
[0,1]
as follows :
for every
0\leq s<t\leq 1
, we set

s\simeq^{Z}t\qquad\text{if}\qquad t=\inf\left\{u>s:Z_{u}\leq Z_{s-}\right\},

then for
0\leq t<s\leq 1
, we set
s\simeq^{Z}t
if
t\simeq^{Z}s
, and we agree that
s\simeq^{Z}s
for every
s\in[0,1]
. We finally define a subset of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
by

L(Z)\quad\coloneqq\quad\bigcup_{s\simeq^{Z}t}\left[{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}s},{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t}\right].

(54)

Observe that
\mathbb{S}\subset L(Z)
by definition. By using
(H0)
and
(H2)
, it is a simple matter to check that

L(Z)\quad=\quad\overline{\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\simeq^{Z}t\\
s\neq t\end{subarray}}\left[{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}s},{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t}\right]}.

(55)

Note that, if
s\simeq^{Z}t
with
s<t
, we automatically have
Z_{s-}=Z_{t}
.

Comment on notation.

Observe that the definition of the lamination
L(Z)
for càdlàg functions (Eq. (
)) with the above properties differs from the one
for continuous functions (Eq. (
)). Since these two sets of functions are disjoint, this should not create any confusion.

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume that
Z\in{\mathbb{D}}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
satisfies
Z_{1}=0
,
Z_{t}>0
and
\Delta Z_{t}\geq 0
for every
t\in(0,1)
,
(H1)
and
(H3)
. Then the lamination
L(Z)
is a geodesic lamination of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
, called the lamination coded by
Z
.

This can be proved by using the same arguments as
[
Kor14
, Prop. 2.9]
, we leave the details to the reader.
Note that the assumptions
(H0)
and
(H2)
are not required in Lemma
4.1
,
but will be later needed in Proposition
4.7
.

Recall from Section
3.1
that
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
denotes the excursion process,
associated with the Lévy process with characteristic exponent given by (
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Almost surely
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
satisfies
(H0)
,
(H1)
,
(H2)
and
(H3)
.

Proof 4.3
.

We mimic the proof of
[
Kor14
, Proposition 2.10]
.
By the construction of
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
as the Vervaat transform of
X^{\mathrm{br}}
