, and by the absolute continuity relation (
), it is sufficient to prove that analogous properties hold
for the Lévy process
X
. The property
(H0)
follows from the fact that the Lévy measure of
X
is infinite. The properties
(H1)
and
(H2)
are consequences
of the (strong) Markov property of
X
and the fact that
0
is regular for
(-\infty,0)
.

For
(H3)
, we will use the time-reversal property of
X
, which
states that if
t>0
and
\widehat{X}^{(t)}
is the process defined by
\widehat{X}^{(t)}_{s}=X_{t}-X_{(t-s)-}
for
0\leq s<t
and
\widehat{X}^{(t)}_{t}=X_{t}
, then the two processes
(X_{s},0\leq s\leq t)
and
(\widehat{X}^{(t)}_{s},0\leq s\leq t)
have the same law.
Set
S_{t}=\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}X_{s}
. By the time-reversal property of
X
,
it is sufficient to prove that if
q>0
is rational and
T=\inf\{t\geq q:X_{t}>S_{q}\}
, then

X_{T}>S_{q}>X_{T-}
almost surely. This follows from the Markov property at time
q

and the fact that for any
a>0
,
X
jumps a.s. across
a
at its first passage time
above
a
(Lévy processes which can creep upwards
are characterized e.g. in
[
Kyp06
, Theorem 7.11]
;
since the Lévy process we are considering has paths of bounded variations,
a negative drift and no Gaussian component, it cannot creep upwards
and it jumps a.s. across any level
a
at its first passage).

Remark 4.4
.

Almost surely
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
also satisfies the fact that for every
t\in(0,1)
such that
\Delta X^{\mathrm{exc}}(t)>0
, we have
\inf_{[t-\varepsilon,t]}X^{\mathrm{exc}}>X^{\mathrm{exc}}(t-)
for every
0<\varepsilon\leq t
(this follows from the time-reversal property of
X
and the fact that
0
is irregular for
(0,\infty)
), but we will not use this property.

For
c>0
,
we now define
\mathbf{L}_{c}
as

\mathbf{L}_{c}\coloneqq L(X^{\mathrm{exc}}),

where
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
is the Lévy excursion process introduced in Section
3.1
;
see Figure
11
for a simulation of
\mathbf{L}_{5}
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[scale]{Excursion_C5.pdf}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[scale]{Lamination_C5.pdf}\end{array}

Figure 11.
A realization of the Lévy excursion process
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
from Section
3.1
for
c=5

and its associated lamination.
The latter is therefore a realization of the random lamination
\mathbf{L}_{5}
.

In what follows, we first concentrate on the case
c\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and discuss the case
c=\infty
in Section
4.4
.

4.2.
A symmetry result.

As a warm-up, we establish a result that will be useful twice : first, it will allow us to restrict ourselves to the case where
K_{n}\leq n/2
in the proof of Theorem
1.3
(ii), and it will also allow us to immediately lift, for
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
, (i) in Theorem
1.3
to (iii).

Recall that if
P
is a non-crossing partition, singleton blocks of
P
do not appear in
\dot{P}
. We denote by
\hat{P}
the compact subset of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
obtained from
\dot{P}
by adding the vertices
\{e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi k/n},1\leq k\leq n\}
. This definition has its importance in view of the next result.

Lemma 4.5
.

Set
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{i}=\hat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcalbis{t}_{1}^{(n)}\mathcalbis{t}_{2}^{(n)}\cdots\mathcalbis{t}_{i}^{(n)})
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
.
Assume that there exists a random lamination
L
such that the convergence
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{n-K_{n}-1}\rightarrow L
holds in distribution.
Then

\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad L.

Proof 4.6
.

Recall that
\mathfrak{P}_{n}
denotes the set of all non-crossing partition of
[n]
and that
\mathcal{K}(P)
is the Kreweras complement of a non-crossing partition
P
.
Using the visual description of Kreweras complement (Figure
4
),
it is a simple matter to see that
\max_{P\in\mathfrak{P}_{n}}d_{H}\big(\hat{P},\widehat{\mathcal{K}(P)}\big)\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. As a consequence,

\widehat{\mathcal{K}\big(\mathcal{P}^{(n)}_{n-K_{n}-1}\big)}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad L.

But by Lemma
2.7
,
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{P}^{(n)}_{n-K_{n}-1})
have the same distribution. This entails the desired result.

4.3.
Convergence to
\mathbf{L}_{c}
for
c>0
and proof of Theorem
1.3
(i)
c>0
.

The goal of this subsection is to prove Theorem
1.3
(i)
c>0
,
which deals with the case
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c>0
.
Recall that if
\tau
is a tree,
(\overline{B}^{n}_{i}(\tau))_{1\leq i\leq|\tau|}
is the Łukasiewicz path of the reduced black subtree of
\tau
, as introduced in Section
3.2
. We also set
\overline{B}^{n}_{i}(\tau)=0
for
i\leq 0
or
i>|\tau|
.

For every
n\geq 1
, we let
(t_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,t_{n-1}^{(n)})\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}
be a minimal factorization. If
1\leq K_{n}\leq n-1
, to simplify notation we set

\mathcal{F}_{n}=\mathcal{F}\big(t_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,t_{K_{n}}^{(n)}\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{P}_{n}=\mathcal{P}\big(t_{1}^{(n)}t_{2}^{(n)}\cdots t_{K_{n}}^{(n)}\big),

and let
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
and respectively
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
be their associated compact subsets of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
.
Finally, we denote by
T_{n}=\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
the tree coding the non-crossing partition
\mathcal{P}_{n}
as in Section
2.3.2
.

Roughly speaking, Theorem
1.3
(i)
c>0
follows from the following deterministic convergence result
(the proof of this implication is given at the end of Section
4.3
).

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
Z\in{\mathbb{D}}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
be a càdlàg function satisfying
(H0)
,
(H1)
,
(H2)
and
(H3)

and let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
,
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
and
T_{n}
be as above.
Assume that the following four properties hold.

(B0)

We have
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c>0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

(B1)

The convergence

\left(c\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(T_{n})}{n}\right)_{-1\leq u\leq 1}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(Z_{u})_{-1\leq u\leq 1}

(56)

holds for the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology, where we set
Z_{u}=0
for
u<0
.

(B2)

For
n
sufficiently large,
T_{n}
has no black vertex with at least three children.

(B3)

For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
n_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for every
n\geq n_{0}(\varepsilon)
, the tree
T_{n}
does not contain a black
vertex
v
that has two children, both with at least
\varepsilon\,n
descendants (counting both white and black vertices).

Then we have the convergence

\left(\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(L(Z),L(Z))

(57)

in the Hausdorff topology.

Before tackling the proof of Proposition
4.7
, we state and prove two lemmas. The first one is a simple geometric lemma and the second one concerns approximation properties of the Skorokhod
J_{1}
metric.

Lemma 4.8
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and assume that
(B2)
,
(B3)
hold. Then, for every
n
sufficiently large,

d_{H}\left(\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}\right)\leq 7\varepsilon,

where
d_{H}
denotes the Hausdorff distance.

Proof 4.9
.

Assume that
n
is chosen sufficiently large so that
(B2)
and
(B3)
for our fixed value of
\varepsilon
are in force.
By
(B2)
,
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
is a disjoint union of line segments and of triangles. Also, by Lemma
2.5
,
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}\subset\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
and
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
is obtained from
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
by removing an edge from every triangle. Now, if
\Delta
is a triangle of
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
, it splits the unit circle into three arcs, and by
(B3)
, one of these arcs has length at most
7\varepsilon
. If
{\Delta}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Delta
by removing one of the three edges of
\Delta
, this implies that
d_{H}(\Delta,{\Delta}^{\prime})\leq 7\varepsilon
. The desired result readily follows.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Proposition
4.7
are in force. Fix
0<s<t<1
.
Then the following assertions are equivalent :

(i)

We have
s\simeq^{Z}t
;

(ii)

There exists integers
i_{n}<j_{n}
such that
\tfrac{i_{n}}{n-K_{n}}\rightarrow s
,
\tfrac{j_{n}}{n-K_{n}}\rightarrow t
and

j_{n}=\inf\left\{k>i_{n}:\,\overline{B}^{n}_{k}(T_{n})=\overline{B}^{n}_{i_{n}}(T_{n})-1\right\}.

Proof 4.11
.

The implication
(i)\implies(ii)
follows from standard approximation properties of the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology (see e.g. the proof of Lemma 4.6. in
[
KM17
]
).

Now assume (ii). To simplify notation, set
\widehat{B}^{(n)}(u)=c\tfrac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(T_{n})}{n}
, so that

\widehat{B}^{(n)}(r)\geq\widehat{B}^{(n)}\left(\frac{i_{n}}{n-K_{n}}\right)=\widehat{B}^{(n)}\left(\frac{j_{n}}{n-K_{n}}\right)+\frac{c}{n}\quad\textrm{for every }r\in\left[\frac{i_{n}}{n-K_{n}},\frac{j_{n}}{n-K_{n}}\right).

(58)

First assume that
Z_{s}=Z_{s-}
.
Then
\widehat{B}^{(n)}\big(\tfrac{i_{n}}{n-K_{n}}\big)\rightarrow Z_{s}

and it follows from (
) and from
(B1)
that
Z_{r}\geq Z_{t-}=Z_{s}
for every
r\in(s,t)
.
In addition, we claim that
Z_{t-}=Z_{t}
.
Indeed, if
Z_{t-}<Z_{t}
and if
Z_{u}\geq Z_{s}
for
u
belonging to a small neighborhood of the left of
s
,
then
(H1)
is not fulfilled.
On the other hand, if
Z_{t-}<Z_{t}
and if
s=\sup\{u\in[0,t]:Z_{u}<Z_{t-}\}
, then
(H3)

is not fulfilled.
Therefore, we must have
Z_{t-}=Z_{t}
.
Similarly, we necessarily have
Z_{r}>Z_{t}
for every
r\in(s,t)
.
Indeed, assuming
Z_{r}=Z_{t}
yields a contradiction with either
(H1)
or
(H3)
,
using the same case distinction as above.
We conclude that
s\simeq^{Z}t
.

Next assume that
Z_{s}>Z_{s-}
. Then, by
(H2)
, we can find
r\in(s,t)
such that
Z_{r}<Z_{s}
.
As a consequence,
\widehat{B}^{(n)}\big(\tfrac{i_{n}}{n-K_{n}}\big)\rightarrow Z_{s-}

(it cannot converge to
Z_{s}
because of (
)), and
Z_{t-}=Z_{s-}
.
Similarly as above, we can prove that
Z_{t}=Z_{t-}
.
We thus have
Z_{s-}=Z_{t}\leq Z_{r}
for every
r\in(s,t)
,
and this inequality is strict (otherwise, as above, this would contradict either
(H1)
or
(H3)
).
Therefore
s\simeq^{Z}t
, and this completes the proof.

We are now ready to establish Proposition
4.7
.

Proof 4.12

(Proof of Proposition
4.7
)
.

Since the set of all geodesic laminations of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
equipped with Hausdorff topology is compact, up to extraction,
we may assume that
(\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n})
converges to a pair of geodesic laminations
(K,K^{\prime})
of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
and we aim at proving that
(K,K^{\prime})=(L(Z),L(Z))
. By Lemma
4.8
,
K=K^{\prime}
and it is therefore enough to show that
K=L(Z)
. Denote by
(v^{\bullet,n}_{i})_{0\leq i<n-K_{n}}
the black vertices of
T_{n}
listed in lexicographical order.

First note that there can not exist
s\in(0,1)
such that
0\simeq^{Z}s
,
and since
Z_{t}>0
for
t\in(0,1)
, there does not exist
s\in(0,1)
such that
s\simeq^{Z}1
either.

Step
1
:
L(Z)\subset K
.

Fix
0<s\leq t<1
such that
s\simeq^{Z}t
.
We need to prove that
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,s},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,t}]\subset K
.
By (
), it is enough to prove it for
s<t
.
Since
K
is a limit point of the sequence
(\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n})_{n\geq 0}

it is enough to check that for fixed
\varepsilon>0
, for every
n
sufficiently large,
there exists a chord of
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
within distance
12\varepsilon
of the chord
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,s},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,t}]
.

By Lemma
4.10
, for
n
sufficiently large, there exist integers
i_{n},j_{n}
such that

\left|\frac{i_{n}}{n-K_{n}}-s\right|\leq\varepsilon,\quad\left|\frac{j_{n}}{n-K_{n}}-t\right|\leq\varepsilon,\quad j_{n}=\inf\left\{k>i_{n}:\,\overline{B}^{n}_{k}(T_{n})=\overline{B}^{n}_{i_{n}}(T_{n})-1\right\}.

(59)

Note that the last equality means that
v^{\bullet,n}_{j_{n}}
is the first vertex greater (in the lexicographical order) than
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
and than all the descendants of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
.

We now find a chord of
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
which is within distance
12\varepsilon
of the chord
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,s},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,t}]
as follows. Denote by
x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
(resp.
y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
) the label of the first (resp. the last) corner of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
visited by the contour sequence.
By construction (see in Section
2.3.2
how the partition
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is reconstructed from
T_{n}
),
we have
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}{x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}/{n}},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}{y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}/{n}}]\in\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
.
Lemma
2.9
(iii) then implies that

i_{n}\leq x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}\leq i_{n}+K_{n}+1,\qquad i_{n}+n^{\bullet}_{i_{n}}\leq y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}\leq i_{n}+n^{\bullet}_{i_{n}}+2K_{n}+2,

(60)

where
n^{\bullet}_{i_{n}}
is the number of black descendants of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
.
But, because of (
), the black vertices visited between
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
and
v^{\bullet,n}_{j_{n}}

in the contour walk of
T_{n}
are exactly the black descendants of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
,
so that
n^{\bullet}_{i_{n}}=j_{n}-i_{n}-1
. Thus

i_{n}\leq x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}\leq i_{n}+K_{n}+1,\qquad j_{n}-1\leq y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}\leq j_{n}-1+2K_{n}+2.

Since
K_{n}\sim c\sqrt{n}
, this implies that for every
n
sufficiently large,

\left|\frac{x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}{n}-s\right|\leq 2\varepsilon,\qquad\left|\frac{y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}{n}-t\right|\leq 2\varepsilon.

As a consequence, for
n
sufficiently large,
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}{x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}/{n}},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}{y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}/{n}}]\in\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
is within distance
12\varepsilon
of the chord
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,s},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,t}]
. This shows that
L(Z)\subset K
.

Step
2
:
K\subset L(Z)
.

Since
K
is a geodesic lamination (i.e. a union of non crossing chords) and since
L(Z)
contains the circle
\mathbb{S}
,
it is enough to show that nontrivial chords of
K
are included in
L(Z)
.
Namely, we need to prove that if
s<t
are such that
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,s},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,t}]\subset K
,
then
s\simeq^{Z}t
.
By definition,
K
is a limit point of
(\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n})_{n\geq 0}

so, up to extraction, we may assume that there exist sequences
(s_{n})
and
(t_{n})

such that
s_{n}\rightarrow s
,
t_{n}\rightarrow t
,
ns_{n}
and
nt_{n}
are integers, and
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,s_{n}},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,t_{n}}]\subset\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
.
By construction,
the condition
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,s_{n}},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}\,t_{n}}]\subset\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}

means that

ns_{n}
and
nt_{n}
are the labels of two consecutive corners of the same black vertex of
T_{n}
;
call
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
this black vertex.
Combining this with the hypothesis
(B2)
, there are two cases :

v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
may have 1 or 2 children, but not more.

Let
ns^{\prime}_{n}
(resp.
nt^{\prime}_{n}
) is the index of the first (resp. last) black corner of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
visited by the contour sequence (if
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
has one child, we simply have
(s_{n},t_{n})=(s^{\prime}_{n},t^{\prime}_{n})
).
By
(B3)
, for
n\geq n_{0}(\varepsilon)
, only one of the quantities
t^{\prime}_{n}-t_{n}
,
t_{n}-s_{n}
and
s_{n}-s^{\prime}_{n}

can be bigger than
\varepsilon
.
Since
t>s
, for
\varepsilon
small enough and
n
sufficiently large, we have
t_{n}-s_{n}>\varepsilon
.
Therefore
t^{\prime}_{n}-t_{n}
and
s_{n}-s^{\prime}_{n}
tend to
0
and
we have
(s^{\prime}_{n},t^{\prime}_{n})\rightarrow(s,t)
.
Note that we
crucially
need
(B3)
to rule out the possibility that
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
has two white children both having a macroscopic descendance, in which case the last convergence may fail.
Then, by Lemma
2.9
(iii),

i_{n}\leq ns^{\prime}_{n}\leq i_{n}+K_{n}+1,\qquad i_{n}+n^{\bullet}_{i_{n}}\leq nt^{\prime}_{n}\leq i_{n}+n^{\bullet}_{i_{n}}+2K_{n}+2,

where
n^{\bullet}_{i_{n}}
is the number of black descendants of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
.
Hence, since
K_{n}/n
tends to
0
, we have
i_{n}/(n-K_{n})\rightarrow s
and
(i_{n}+n^{\bullet}_{i_{n}})/(n-K_{n})\rightarrow t
.
Besides, by construction of
\overline{B}
, it holds that

i_{n}+n^{\bullet}_{i_{n}}+1=\min\{k>i_{n}:\overline{B}^{n}_{k}(T_{n})=\overline{B}^{n}_{i_{n}}(T_{n})-1\}.

Lemma
4.10
implies that
s\simeq^{Z}t
, and this completes the proof.

We now conclude the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i) in the case
c>0
.

Proof 4.13

(Proof of Theorem
1.3
(i) in the case
c>0
)
.

Since the space of laminations of the disk is compact,
it is enough to show that the sequence
\big(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}},\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\big)
has a unique limit point in distribution. Up to extraction, we may therefore assume that
\big(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}},\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\big)
converges in distribution. We shall show that the limiting distribution is the law of
(\mathbf{L}_{c},\mathbf{L}_{c})
.

For each
n\geq 1
, we denote by
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}=\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcalbis{t}_{1}^{(n)}\mathcalbis{t}_{2}^{(n)}\cdots\mathcalbis{t}_{K_{n}}^{(n)}))

the tree associated with
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
.
From Proposition
2.11
, it has the distribution of an alternating BGW tree with our favorite offspring distributions
and a modified offspring distribution at the root (as in Theorem
3.10
(ii)).
We consider the following events :

—

for
\varepsilon>0
, we let
A_{n}(\varepsilon)
be the event “the tree
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
does not contain a black
vertex
v
that has two children, both with at least
\varepsilon\,n
descendants” ;

—

B_{n}
is the event “
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
does not contain any black vertex with three or more children”.

From Lemmas
3.21
and
3.25
combined with Lemma
3.19
, we know that
as
n\rightarrow\infty
,
\mathbb{P}\left(B_{n}\right)\rightarrow 0
and, for each
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathbb{P}\left(A_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)\rightarrow 0
as well.
Therefore, we can find a subsequence
(\phi(n))_{n\geq 1}
such that, for every
n\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}\left({B_{\phi(n)}}\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{2^{n}},\qquad\mathbb{P}\left({A_{\phi(n)}(\tfrac{1}{n})}\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{2^{n}}.

The Borel-Cantelli lemma ensures the almost sure convergence of

\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\phi(n)}}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\phi(n)}(1/n)}
to
0
.
On the other hand, from Theorem
3.10
(ii), we know that the following convergence in distribution holds

\left(c\frac{\overline{B}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n})}{n}:-1\leq u\leq 1\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(X^{\mathrm{exc}}_{u}:-1\leq u\leq 1).

The three previous convergences hold jointly in distribution, so that by Skorokhod representation theorem,
we can find a coupling of
(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{\phi(n)})_{n\geq 1}
such that these convergences hold almost surely.
The sequence of associated random factorizations almost surely satisfies the assumptions

(B1)
,
(B2)
and
(B3)
of Proposition
4.7
.
Besides, from Lemma
4.2
, the random process
X^{\mathrm{exc}}
almost surely
satisfies conditions
(H0)
,
(H1)
,
(H2)
and
(H3)
.
From Proposition
4.7
,
we conclude that
\big(\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(\phi(n))}_{K_{\phi(n)}},\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(\phi(n))}_{K_{\phi(n)}}\big)
converges in distribution
to
(\mathbf{L}_{c},\mathbf{L}_{c})
, since
\mathbf{L}_{c}=L(X^{\mathrm{exc}})
by definition.
Therefore the limiting distribution of the whole sequence
\big(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}},\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\big)

must be
(\mathbf{L}_{c},\mathbf{L}_{c})
and this completes the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i) in the case
c>0
.

4.4.
Convergence to
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
and proof of Theorem
1.3
(ii)

Our goal is now to establish Theorem
1.3
(ii), when both
\frac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\frac{n-K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
The main difference with the case
\frac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c>0
occurs when we assume
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in(0,1)
.
In the latter case, both the number of white and black vertices then have a positive proportion in the dual tree coding
\mathcal{P}(\mathcalbis{t}_{1}^{(n)}\mathcalbis{t}_{2}^{(n)}\cdots\mathcalbis{t}_{K_{n}}^{(n)})
.

As before,
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
will denote the probability distributions satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.8
.
We also set
\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{\bullet}(i)=\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(i-1)
for
i\geq 1
.
We let
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}
be a random tree with distribution
{\textrm{BGW}}^{\widetilde{\mu}_{\bullet}^{n},\mu_{\bullet}^{n},\mu_{\circ}^{n}}
, as defined in (
).
In words,
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}
is
an alternating two-type BGW tree (with a black root), with offspring distributions
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
except the root which has offspring distribution
\widetilde{\mu}^{n}_{\bullet}
.
We then define
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
, as
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}
conditioned on having
n-K_{n}
black vertices and
K_{n}+1
white vertices.
Recall from the beginning of Section
3.2
that we associate
two paths
(\overline{B}^{n}_{i}(T))_{0\leq i\leq n-K_{n}}
and
(\overline{H}^{n}_{i}(T))_{0\leq i\leq n-K_{n}}
with each alternating tree
T

with
n-K_{n}
black vertices.

We first give the analogue of Theorem
3.10
in the setting
c=\infty
.
In the following statement, as in Section
4.1
,

\mathbbm{e}
is the Brownian excursion.
We also use the notation of Lemma
2.9
and Section
3.2
.
In particular,
we denote by
(v^{\bullet,n}_{i})_{0\leq i<n-K_{n}}
are the black vertices
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
, listed in lexicographical order,
and, for
0\leq i<n-K_{n}
,
we let
\ell^{\bullet,n}_{i}
be the number of black corners
branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,v^{\bullet,n}_{i}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
for
0\leq i<n-K_{n}
.

Proposition 4.14
.

Assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in[0,1)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Set
D^{\circ}_{n}=\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}}
. Then

\left(\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n})}{D^{\circ}_{n}}:0\leq u\leq 1\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\mathbbm{e}.

In addition, if
\gamma>0
, then

\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\left|\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n})}{K_{n}}-u\right|\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(\mathbb{P})}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\frac{1}{n}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\ell^{\bullet,n}_{i}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(\mathbb{P})}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0.

The condition
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in(0,1)
means the both the number of white and black vertices have a positive proportion in
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
. The proof of Proposition
4.14
, very similar in spirit to that of Theorem
3.10
, is postponed to the end of Section
4.4.2
.

In the next Section
4.4.1
, we develop deterministic tools that allow us to show that Proposition
4.14
implies Theorem
1.3
(ii).

4.4.1.
Convergence to the Brownian triangulation

As in Section
4.3
, for every
n\geq 1
, we let
(t_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,t_{n-1}^{(n)})\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}
be a minimal factorization. If
1\leq K_{n}\leq n-1
, we set

\mathcal{F}_{n}=\mathcal{F}\big(t_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,t_{K_{n}}^{(n)}\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{P}_{n}=\mathcal{P}\big(t_{1}^{(n)}t_{2}^{(n)}\cdots t_{K_{n}}^{(n)}\big)

and we denote by
T_{n}=\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
the tree coding the non-crossing partition
\mathcal{P}_{n}
as in Section
2.3.2
.

Our goal is to prove some convergence result for the pair
\big(\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}\big)
,
but it turns out to be enough to consider

\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
, as ensured by the following lemma.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
Z\in{\mathbb{C}}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
be a continuous function with
Z_{0}=Z_{1}=0
satisfying
(C1)
.
We assume that
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}\rightarrow L(Z)
as
n
tends to infinity.
Then we also have
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}\rightarrow L(Z)
.

Proof 4.16
.

Since the set of all geodesic laminations of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
equipped with Hausdorff topology is compact, we may assume that the convergence
\big(\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}\big)\rightarrow(K,L(Z))
holds, where
K
is a lamination, and we aim at showing that
K=L(Z)
. By maximality of
L(Z)
, it is enough to show that
L(Z)\subset K
.
Consider a chord
\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t}\big]
of
L(Z)
(with
s\neq t
),
we want to show that
\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t}\big]\subset K
.

By (
), for every
\varepsilon>0
, we can find another chord
\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s^{\prime}},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t^{\prime}}\big]\subset L(Z)
such that
0<|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon
and
0<|t-t^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon
.
By symmetry, we can assume that
s<s^{\prime}<t^{\prime}<t
.
The following argument having some geometric flavor,
looking at Figure
12
while reading the proof might help.

These two chords form a region of the disk denoted by
R
.
Again by (
), there exists a chord
\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi u},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi v}\big]\subset L(Z)
such that
s<u<s^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}<v<t
.
This implies that for each
\eta>0
small enough and
n
larger to some threshold
n_{0}(\eta)
,
we can find a block
\dot{B}_{n}
of
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
such that :

—

\dot{B}_{n}
has a chord with endpoints at distance at most
\eta
of

e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi u}
and
e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi v}
;

—

moreover, all vertices of
\dot{B}_{n}
are in the
\eta
enlargement
of one of the arcs
(e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s^{\prime}})
or

(e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t^{\prime}})
(we denote arcs with parentheses to
distinguish them from chords).

We recall that
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
is obtained from
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
by replacing each polygon
by a spanning tree of its vertices. It is clear, that for the block
B_{n}

with the above property, such a spanning tree must contain a chord that
has one extremity on each side, i.e. one in the
\eta
enlargement
of
(e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s^{\prime}})

and one in the
\eta
enlargement
of
(e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t^{\prime}})
.
Therefore
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
contains a chord at distance
\varepsilon+\eta

of our original chord
\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t}\big]
.
Since this is true for all
\varepsilon
and
\eta
sufficiently small,
this implies that
\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t}\big]\subset K
and shows that
K=L(Z)
.

Figure 12.
An illustration of the second part of the proof. The region
R
is in gray,
the block
\dot{B}_{n}
is in dashed lines.
This block is entirely contained in a small enlargement of
R

and has at least one chord joining a small enlargement of the
(e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s^{\prime}})
arc
with that of the
(e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t^{\prime}})
arc.
No matter which spanning tree of the vertices of
\dot{B}_{n}
is present in
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}

it will contain a chord close to
\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t}\big]
.

Let us point out that, although the above lemma is similar in spirit to Lemma
4.8

(both show that
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
and
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
are in some sense close),
the arguments are quite different.
One the one hand, Lemma
3.25
, which is the base of Lemma
4.8
,
has certainly no analogue when there is a constant proportion of vertices of both colors in the tree.
On the other hand, here, we need to assume the convergence of
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
to some lamination
encoded by a
continuous function
satisfying
(C1)
to prove that it is close to
\dot{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
.

The gist of the proof of Theorem
1.3
(ii) is contained in the next result.
The notation is the same as in Proposition
4.14
.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
Z\in{\mathbb{C}}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
be a continuous function such that
Z_{0}=Z_{1}=0
satisfying
(C1)
. For every
n\geq 1
, let
(t_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,t_{n-1}^{(n)})\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}
be a minimal factorization, and denote by
T_{n}=\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
the tree coding the non-crossing partition
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\mathcal{P}(t_{1}^{(n)},\ldots,t_{K_{n}}^{(n)})
. Assume that the following two properties hold.

(D0)

We have
\frac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in[0,1)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

(D1)

There exists a sequence
D^{\circ}_{n}\rightarrow\infty
such that

\left(\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(T_{n})}{D^{\circ}_{n}}:0\leq u\leq 1\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad Z.

(D2)

If
\gamma>0
, we require that

\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\left|\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(T_{n})}{K_{n}}-u\right|\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\frac{1}{n}\max_{0\leq i<n-K_{n}}\ell^{\bullet,n}_{i}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0.

Then we have the convergence

\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad L(Z)

(61)

in the Hausdorff topology.

Before proving Proposition
4.17
, let us explain how Theorem
1.3
(ii) follows from a combination of Propositions
4.14
and
4.17
and Lemmas
4.5
and
4.15
.

Proof 4.18

(Proof of Theorem
1.3
(ii))
.

First assume that
\frac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and that
K_{n}\leq\frac{n}{2}
for every
n\geq 1
. Up to extraction, we can suppose that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
. By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we may also suppose that the convergence in Proposition
4.14
holds almost surely. Then, by Proposition
4.17
, the convergence

\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\mathbf{L}_{\infty},

holds almost surely. In turn, by Lemma
4.15
, this implies that the joint convergence

\left(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}},\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(\mathbf{L}_{\infty},\mathbf{L}_{\infty})

holds almost surely, and the desired conclusion follows.

Now assume that
\frac{n-K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
K_{n}>\frac{n}{2}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
The previous paragraph tells us that

\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{n-K_{n}-1}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\mathbf{L}_{\infty}.

Lemma
4.5
entails that the convergence
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
holds in distribution. As a consequence, since
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
is obtained from
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
by adding points of
\mathbb{S}
, any sub-sequential distributional limit of
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
must contain all the non-trivial chords of
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
, and by (
) must be equal to
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
. In other words, the convergence
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
holds in distribution, and another application of Lemma
4.15
implies that the joint convergence

\left(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}},\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(\mathbf{L}_{\infty},\mathbf{L}_{\infty})

holds in distribution. This completes the proof.

Proof 4.19

(Proof of Proposition
4.17
)
.

Since the set of all geodesic laminations of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
equipped with Hausdorff topology is compact, up to extraction, we may therefore assume that
\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
converges to a geodesic lamination
K
of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
and we aim at proving that
K=L(Z)
.
By maximality, it is enough to check that
L(Z)\subseteq K
.
By (
), it suffices to show that if
s\sim^{Z}t
and if
s
nor
t
are local minima of
Z
, with
0<s<t<
, then
\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t}\big]\subset K
.
Using the convergence
(D1)
, similarly as in Eq. (
), we can find black vertices

v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
and
v^{\bullet,n}_{j_{n}}
in
T_{n}

such that :

—

i_{n}/(n-K_{n})\rightarrow s
and
j_{n}/(n-K_{n})\rightarrow t
;

—

all the black vertices between
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
and
v^{\bullet,n}_{j_{n}}

in the lexicographical order are descendants of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
.

Denote by
x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
(resp.
y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
)
the label of the first (resp. the last) corner of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
visited by the contour sequence. We claim that

\frac{x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}{n}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad s,\qquad\frac{y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}{n}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad t.

(62)

Since
[e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}{x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}/{n}},e^{-2\pi\,\textrm{i}{y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}/{n}}]\subset\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
, this will then imply
\big[e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi s},e^{-2\textrm{i}\pi t}\big]\subset K
and the proof will be complete.

First case :
\gamma=0
.

By Lemma
2.9
(iii),
we have
i_{n}\leq x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}\leq i_{n}+K_{n}+1
and

x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}+(j_{n}-i_{n}-1)\leq y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}\leq x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}+(i_{n}-j_{n}-1)+2K_{n}+2,

since the number of black descendants of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
is
j_{n}-i_{n}-1
.
But we have
{K_{n}}/{n}\rightarrow 0
,
i_{n}/(n-K_{n})\rightarrow s
and
j_{n}/(n-K_{n})\rightarrow t
,
which implies (
).

Second case :
\gamma>0
.

In this case, the number of white vertices is no longer negligible, and we use the precise identity of Lemma
2.9
(ii). Recalling that
k_{j}
is the number of children of
v^{\bullet,n}_{j}

and that
\ell_{i_{n}}^{\bullet,n}
denotes the number of black corners
branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
,
we have

x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}=i_{n}+\sum_{j=0}^{i_{n}-1}k_{j}-\ell_{i_{n}}^{\bullet,n}=i_{n}+\overline{H}^{n}_{i_{n}}(T_{n})-\ell_{i_{n}}^{\bullet,n}.

(63)

Since
i_{n}/(n-K_{n})\rightarrow s
and
K_{n}\sim\gamma n
, using
(D2)
, we get

i_{n}+\overline{H}^{n}_{i_{n}}(T_{n})+\ell_{i_{n}}^{\bullet,n}=s(1-\gamma)n+s\gamma n+o(n)=sn+o(n).

(64)

This proves that
\tfrac{x^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}{n}\rightarrow s
.

In order to show that
\tfrac{y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}}{n}\rightarrow t
, note that the number of black descendants of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}
is again
j_{n}-i_{n}-1
while its number of white descendants is
\overline{H}^{n}_{j_{n}}(T_{n})-\overline{H}^{n}_{i_{n}}(T_{n})
. Hence, by Lemma
2.9
(i) and using
(D2)
, we get

y^{\bullet,n}_{i_{n}}=x^{n}_{i_{n}}+\big(j_{n}-i_{n}-1\big)+\big(\overline{H}^{n}_{j_{n}}(T_{n})-\overline{H}^{n}_{i_{n}}(T_{n})\big)=j_{n}+\overline{H}^{n}_{j_{n}}(T_{n})-\ell_{i_{n}}^{\bullet,n}-1=tn+o(n),

where the last equality is obtained exactly as before. This completes the proof.

4.4.2.
Proof of the technical results

We now turn to the proof of Proposition
4.14
.
The strategy is similar to the case
c>0
: we first establish convergence of unconditioned processes using local limit theorems, then lift them to the bridge version by absolute continuity and finally conclude by using a Vervaat transform.

As before, we let

\mu_{\circ}^{n}
and
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
be the offspring distributions defined in Lemma
3.8
(i), and we let
S^{\circ,n}_{k}
,
S^{\bullet,n}_{k}
denote respectively the sum of
k
i.i.d. random variables distributed according to
\mu_{\circ}^{n},\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
.

Local limit theorems.

We first state the analogue of Lemmas
3.14
and
3.15
in our new regime.
First set

D^{\bullet}_{n,N}\coloneqq\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}N},\qquad D^{\circ}_{n}\coloneqq\sqrt{(\sigma_{\circ}^{n})^{2}K_{n}},

and
D^{\bullet}_{n}\coloneqq D^{\bullet}_{n,n}=\sqrt{(\sigma_{\bullet}^{n})^{2}n}
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Assume that
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in[0,1)
.
For every
u\in(0,1]
,

\sup_{un\leq N\leq n}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|D^{\bullet}_{n,N}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{N}=k\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{k-N\cdot\frac{K_{n}+1}{n-K_{n}}}{D^{\bullet}_{n,N}}\right)^{2}\right)\right|\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0

(65)

and

\hskip-9.95845pt\sup_{|j|\leq K_{n}^{3/4}}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|D^{\circ}_{n}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{uK_{n}+j}=k\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi u}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2u}\left(\frac{k-(uK_{n}+j)\frac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)^{2}\right)\right|\ \mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ 0.

(66)

Proof 4.21
.

We only give the main steps, since the structure of the proof is very similar to those of Lemmas
3.14
and
3.15
. One separately treats two cases. The first one is
\gamma>0
. In this case,
b_{\bullet}^{n}
and
b_{\circ}^{n}
converge to values belonging to
(0,1/e)
. In particular, there are no singularities, and one may follow the standard proof of the local limit theorem (see e.g.
[
Dur10
, Theorem 3.5.2]
). The second case is
\gamma=0
, in which case
D^{\bullet}_{n}\sim\sqrt{K_{n}}
and
D^{\circ}_{n}\sim n^{3/2}/K_{n}
. The first estimate (
) in contained in Lemma
3.14
. For the second estimate, the main idea is to write, as in the proof of Lemma
3.27
,

\phi^{\circ}_{n}(t)=1+\textrm{i}\mathbb{E}[S^{\circ,n}_{1}]t-\mathbb{E}[(S^{\circ,n}_{1})^{2}]\frac{t^{2}}{2}+r^{n}(t),

where
|r^{n}(t)|\leq|t|^{3}\mathbb{E}[(S^{\circ,n}_{1})^{3}]/6
for every
n\geq 1
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular, by using the expansion of
F(1/e-z)
around
z=0
, one sees that
\mathbb{E}[(S^{\circ,n}_{1})^{3}]\sim 3(n/K_{n})^{5}
, so that for every
n
sufficiently large and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
|r^{n}(t/D^{\circ}_{n})|\leq|t|^{3}\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{K_{n}^{2}}
, and the key point is that
\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{K_{n}^{2}}=o(\tfrac{1}{K_{n}})
since
K_{n}/\sqrt{n}\rightarrow\infty
. From this follows an analogue of Lemma
3.30
, namely that there exist constants
A_{0},\varepsilon,\kappa>0
such that for every
n
sufficiently large and for every
|t|
in
(A_{0},\varepsilon D^{\circ}_{n})
, we have
|\phi^{\circ}_{n}(\tfrac{t}{D^{\circ}_{n}})|\leq e^{-\kappa\frac{t^{2}}{K_{n}}}
. This then allows us to follow the same steps as the proof of Lemma
3.15
. We leave the details to the reader.

Convergence of the unconditioned processes.

Recall that
(H^{n}_{k},B^{n}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
denotes a sequence of i.i.d. random variables with distribution given by : for
i,j\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\big(H^{n}=i,B^{n}=j\big)=\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(i)\,\mathbb{P}\big(S^{\circ,n}_{i}=j\big).

Also, for
i\geq 0
, we set
\overline{H}^{n}_{i}=H^{n}_{1}+H^{n}_{2}+\cdots+H^{n}_{i}
and
\overline{B}^{n}_{i}=B^{n}_{1}+B^{n}_{2}+\cdots+B^{n}_{i}-i
.
To simplify notation, set

\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}=\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}-u(K_{n}+1)}{D^{\bullet}_{n}},\qquad\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{u}=\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}-\frac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}}{D^{\circ}_{n}}.

Lemma 4.22
.

Assume that
\frac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in[0,1)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
The following convergence holds in distribution in
\mathbb{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

\left(\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u},\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{u}\right)_{0\leq u\leq 1}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(W_{u},X_{u})_{0\leq u\leq 1},

where
W,X
are two independent standard Brownian motions.

As the proof will show, the appearance of the normalized quantity
\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}
is important in the definition of
\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{u}
.

Proof 4.23
.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
3.16
. In virtue of
[
Kal02
, Theorem 16.14]
, it is enough to check that the one-dimensional convergence holds for
u=1
. For fixed
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, by Lemma
3.5
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=\lfloor xD^{\bullet}_{n}+K_{n}\rfloor,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=\lfloor yD^{\circ}_{n}+\frac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}\lfloor xD^{\bullet}_{n}\rfloor\rfloor\right)\\
=\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n-K_{n}}=\lfloor xD^{\bullet}_{n}+K_{n}\rfloor\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{\lfloor xD^{\bullet}_{n}+K_{n}\rfloor}=n-K_{n}+\left\lfloor yD^{\circ}_{n}+\frac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}\lfloor xD^{\bullet}_{n}\rfloor\right\rfloor\right).

By (
) and (
), as
n\rightarrow\infty
, this quantity is asymptotic to
\tfrac{1}{D^{\bullet}_{n}}p(x)\cdot\tfrac{1}{D^{\circ}_{n}}p(y)
,
where
p(x)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-{x^{2}}/{2}}
denotes the Gaussian density.
It is then standard (see e.g.
[
Bil68
, Theorem 7.8]
) that this implies that the convergence

\left(\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{1},\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}-\frac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{1}}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(W_{1},X_{1}),

holds in distribution. This completes the proof.

Convergence of the bridge version.

The proof of the following lemma is similar to that of Proposition
3.11
and is left to the reader.

Lemma 4.24
.

Assume that
\frac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in[0,1)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Conditionally given the event

\{\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=K_{n}+1,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=-1\}
, the following convergence in distribution holds jointly

\left(\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u},\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{u}\right)_{0\leq u\leq 1}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad(W^{\mathrm{br}},X^{\mathrm{br}}),

where
W^{\mathrm{br}}
and
X^{\mathrm{br}}
are two independent Brownian bridges.

Corollary 4.25
.

Assume that, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, we have
\frac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma

with
\gamma
in
[0,1)
. Conditionally given the event

\{\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=K_{n}+1,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=-1\}
, the following convergence in distribution holds jointly

\left(\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\right)_{0\leq u\leq 1}\ \mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ X^{\mathrm{br}},\qquad\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\left|\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}}{K_{n}}-u\right|\ \mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ 0.

Proof 4.26
.

Since
\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}

converges in distribution and since
\frac{1}{D^{\circ}_{n}}\cdot\frac{n-K_{n}}{K_{n}+1}\rightarrow 0
, we have that

\frac{n-K_{n}}{D^{\circ}_{n}(K_{n}+1)}\cdot\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}|\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}|\rightarrow 0.

Therefore,
\frac{\overline{B}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}}{D^{\circ}_{n}}

has the same limit as
\widehat{B}^{(n)}_{u}
, namely
X^{\mathrm{br}}
.

For the second convergence, we simply write

\frac{\overline{H}^{n}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}}{K_{n}}-u=\frac{D^{\bullet}_{n}}{K_{n}}\cdot\widehat{H}^{(n)}_{u}

and the result follows from the fact that
\frac{D^{\bullet}_{n}}{K_{n}}\rightarrow 0
.

Convergence of the excursion version.

Using the (bivariate) Vervaat transform and Lemma
3.3
,
we can now state an invariance principle for
\overline{B}^{n}
and
\overline{H}^{n}

conditionally given the event

\mathcal{C}^{+}=\{\overline{H}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=K_{n}+1,\,\overline{B}^{n}_{n-K_{n}}=-1,\,\overline{B}^{n}_{i}\geq 0\text{ for all }i<n-K_{n}\}.

From Lemma
3.1
,
under this conditioning
(\overline{B}^{n},\overline{H}^{n})
has the same distribution
as
(\overline{B}(\mathscr{T}_{n}),\overline{H}(\mathscr{T}_{n}))
,
where
\mathscr{T}_{n}
is an alternating BGW tree with offspring distributions
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}

conditioned on having
n-K_{n}
black vertices and
K_{n}+1
white vertices.
We will thus state our invariance principle in terms of
(\overline{B}(\mathscr{T}_{n}),\overline{H}(\mathscr{T}_{n}))
.

Proposition 4.27
.

Assume that
\frac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in[0,1)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. The following convergences hold jointly in distribution

\left(\frac{\overline{B}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\mathscr{T}_{n})}{D^{\circ}_{n}}:0\leq u\leq 1\right)\ \mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ \mathbbm{e},\qquad\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\left|\frac{\overline{H}_{\lfloor u(n-K_{n})\rfloor}(\mathscr{T}_{n})}{K_{n}}-u\right|\ \mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ 0.

This proposition follows from Corollary
4.25
,
in the same way that Theorem
3.10
(i) was established using Proposition
3.11
.
Again, we leave details to the reader.

End of the proof of Proposition
4.14

We consider separately the cases
\gamma=0
and
\gamma>0
.
The first one is effortless.

Proof 4.28

(Proof of Proposition
4.14
when
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
)
.

In this case, the conclusions of Lemma
3.19
are true with the same proof, where the occurrences of the quantity
p(0)q_{1}(c)
should be replaced by
p(0)^{2}
, and where instead of using the local limit Lemmas
3.14
and
3.15
, one uses (
) and (
). This shows that as before,
\mathscr{T}_{n}
and
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
can be coupled so that
\mathscr{T}_{n}=\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
with probability tending to
1
. Proposition
4.14
then readily follows from Proposition
4.27
.

We now concentrate on the case
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in(0,1)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, which requires new ideas.
In this setting, we set
m_{\bullet}:=\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}
and
m_{\circ}:=\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}
,
which are the limits of the means
m_{\bullet}^{n}
and
m_{\circ}^{n}
, of
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
respectively.
By an argument similar to that used in Lemma
3.8
, there exist unique parameters
(a_{\bullet},b_{\bullet},a_{\circ},b_{\circ})
with
b_{\bullet},b_{\circ}\in(0,1/e)

such that the following equations define probability distributions with means
m_{\bullet}
and
m_{\circ}
:

\mu_{\bullet}(i)=a_{\bullet}\cdot(b_{\bullet})^{i}\cdot\frac{(i+1)^{i-1}}{i!}\quad(i\geq 0),\qquad\mu_{\circ}(i)=a_{\circ}\cdot(b_{\circ})^{i}\cdot\frac{(i+1)^{i-1}}{i!}\quad(i\geq 0).

(67)

It is then immediate that
(a_{\bullet}^{n},b_{\bullet}^{n},a_{\circ}^{n},b_{\circ}^{n})
converges as
n\rightarrow\infty
to the vector
(a_{\bullet},b_{\bullet},a_{\circ},b_{\circ})
.
As a consequence, one easily check that the distributions
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}

have
uniform exponential moments
, in the sense that

\exists\delta>0,\,\qquad\sup_{n\geq 1}\,\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}e^{\delta i}\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(i)<\infty\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sup_{n\geq 1}\,\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}e^{\delta i}\mu_{\circ}^{n}(i)<\infty.

(68)

This result will be useful later.

We first establish a result which will allow us to replace
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
with
\mathscr{T}_{n}
,
that is to forget about the specific offspring distribution of the root.
As before, we denote
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}
the unconditioned version of
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}

with distribution
{\textrm{BGW}}^{\widetilde{\mu}_{\bullet}^{n},\mu_{\bullet}^{n},\mu_{\circ}^{n}}
, as defined in (
).
Recall that the superscript
n
indicates that the offspring distributions depend on
n
, and not a conditioning.

Lemma 4.29
.

The following assertions are satisfied.

(i)

We have

\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}}\rvert=K_{n}+1,\lvert\bullet_{\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}}\rvert=n-K_{n}\right)\ \mathop{\sim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ \left(\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}a\mu_{\bullet}(a-1)\right)\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma_{\bullet}\sigma_{\circ}\gamma\sqrt{\gamma(1-\gamma)}}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{2}},

where
(\sigma_{\bullet})^{2}
,
(\sigma_{\circ})^{2}
denote respectively the variance of
\mu_{\bullet}
,
\mu_{\circ}
.

(ii)

The couple
\big(\overline{H}^{n}_{1}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}),\overline{B}^{n}_{1}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n})\big)
converges in distribution as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

(iii)

Let
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}=(\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{1},\ldots,\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{k})
denote a collection of
k
i.i.d. BGW trees distributed as
\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}
(with a black root). Then, for fixed
i_{0}
, conditionally given
\{\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert=K_{n}+1-i_{0},\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert=n-K_{n}-1\}
, the random variable
\min_{1\leq i\leq k}(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert-\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{i}}\rvert,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert-\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{i}}\rvert)
converges in distribution, where the minimum refers to the lexicographical order on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
.

Proof 4.30
.

We follow the strategy of
[
KM16
, Lemma 5.7]
.
For
j\geq 0
, let
\mathscr{F}^{\circ,n}_{j}
denote a collection of
j
i.i.d. BGW trees distributed as
\mathscr{T}^{\circ,n}
(with a white root). By considering the trees (with white roots) grafted on the root of
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}
and using (
), write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}}\rvert=K_{n}+1,\lvert\bullet_{\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}}\rvert=n-K_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(a-1)\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\circ,n}_{a}}\rvert=K_{n}+1,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\circ,n}_{a}}\rvert=n-K_{n}-1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}a\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(a-1)\frac{1}{K_{n}+1}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n-K_{n}-1}=K_{n}+1-a\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{K_{n}+1}=n-K_{n}-1\right).

The desired result then follows by combining (
) and (
) with the dominated convergence theorem.

For (ii), fix
a,b\geq 1
. Since
(\overline{H}^{n}_{1}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}),\overline{B}^{n}_{1}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}))=(a,b-1)
if and only if the root of
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
has
a
(white) children, which altogether have
b
(black) children, using (
), the quantity
\mathbb{P}\big(\overline{H}^{n}_{1}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}),\overline{B}^{n}_{1}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n})=(a,b-1)\big)
is equal to

\frac{\mu_{\bullet}^{n}(a-1)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{a}=b\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{b}}\rvert=K_{n}+1-a,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{b}}\rvert=n-K_{n}-1\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}}\rvert=K_{n},\lvert\bullet_{\tilde{\mathscr{T}}^{n}}\rvert=n-K_{n}\right)},

where, for
j\geq 1
,
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{j}
denotes a collection of
j
i.i.d. BGW trees distributed as
\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}
(with a black root).
By (
), the quantity
\mathbb{P}\big(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{b}}\rvert=K_{n}+1-a,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{b}}\rvert=n-K_{n}-1\big)
is equal to

\ \frac{b}{n-K_{n}-1}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n-K_{n}-1}=K_{n}+1-a\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{K_{n}+1-a}=n-K_{n}-1-b\right).

By combining (
) and (
) with (i), we finally get that

\mathbb{P}\big(\overline{H}^{n}_{1}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}),\overline{B}^{n}_{1}(\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n})=(a,b-1)\big)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mu_{\bullet}(a-1)b\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ}_{a}=b\right)}{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}i\mu_{\bullet}(i-1)},

where
(S^{\circ}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
is a random walk with jump distribution
\mu_{\circ}
.
Since
\mathbb{E}\left[S^{\circ}_{1}\right]=\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}
, the limiting quantity indeed defines a probability distribution.

For (iii), fix integers
u,v\geq 0
. Observe that for
n
sufficiently large, the following union is disjoint

{

≤
