.

Hence, by exchangeability, the probability of the latter event is

\!\!k\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{1}}\rvert=K_{n}+1-i_{0}-u,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{1}}\rvert=n-K_{n}-1-v\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k-1}}\rvert=u,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k-1}}\rvert=v\right)

Now, using (
),

\frac{k\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{1}}\rvert=K_{n}+1-i_{0}-u,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{1}}\rvert=n-K_{n}-1-v\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert=K_{n}+1-i_{0},\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert=n-K_{n}-1\right)}\\
=\frac{\frac{k}{n-K_{n}-1-v}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n-K_{n}-1-v}=K_{n}+1-i_{0}-u\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{K_{n}+1-i_{0}-u}=n-K_{n}-2-v\right)}{\frac{k}{n-K_{n}-1}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{n-K_{n}-1}=K_{n}+1-i_{0}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\circ,n}_{K_{n}+1-i_{0}}=n-K_{n}-2\right)}.

By (
) and (
), this quantity tends to
1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. By combining Eqs. (
), (
) and (
) we also get that

\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k-1}}\rvert=u,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k-1}}\rvert=v\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}_{k-1}}\rvert=u,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}_{k-1}}\rvert=v\right),

where
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}_{k-1}
denotes a collection of
k-1
i.i.d. alternating BGW trees with offspring distribution
\mu_{\bullet}
and
\mu_{\circ}
and black roots.

We conclude that

\mathbb{P}\left(\min_{1\leq i\leq k}(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert-\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{i}}\rvert,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert-\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{T}^{\bullet,n}_{i}}\rvert)=(u,v)\,\Big|\,\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert=K_{n}+1-i_{0},\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet,n}_{k}}\rvert=n-K_{n}-1\right)

converges to
\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\circ_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}_{k-1}}\rvert=u,\lvert\bullet_{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}_{k-1}}\rvert=v\right)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
This completes the proof.

We are now ready to establish Proposition
4.14
in the final case.

Proof 4.31

(Proof of Proposition
4.14
when
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{n}\rightarrow\gamma\in(0,1)
.)
.

Lemma
4.29
(ii) and (iii) shows that with probability tending to
1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, among all the trees grafted on a (black) grandchild of the root of
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
, only one has
n-K_{n}+o(n)
black vertices and
K_{n}+o(n)
white vertices, while all the others have
o(n)
vertices. It is therefore sufficient to establish the desired result with
\tilde{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
replaced with
\mathscr{T}_{n}
. The first two convergences in Proposition
4.14
are then obtained from Proposition
4.27
.

It remains to check that

\frac{1}{n}\min_{0\leq i<n-K_{n}}\ell^{\bullet,n}_{i}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(\mathbb{P})}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0,

where
\ell^{\bullet,n}_{i}
denotes the number of black corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,v^{\bullet,n}_{i}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
for
0\leq i<n-K_{n}
(recall that
v^{\bullet,n}_{i}
is the
i
-th black vertex of
\mathscr{T}_{n}
in lexicographical order).
The proof is divided in several steps : (i) Reduction to a one-type tree (ii) Reduction to a non-conditioned statement (iii) Large deviation estimates.

Step 1 : reduction to a one-type tree.

w_{2}
w_{1}
\emptyset
w_{1}
w_{2}
z
w_{1}
w_{2}

Figure 13
.
From left to right : a two-type tree
T
with black root, the two-type tree
T^{\circ}
with white root obtained be re-rooting
T
at its first white corner (which is highlighted), and finally its associated reduced white subtree
\hat{T}^{\circ}
. The number of black corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w_{2}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
T
is
3
, which is the same as the number of black corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w_{2}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
T^{\circ}
, and which is less or equal than the number of corners branching to the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w_{2}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
\hat{T^{\circ}}
(which is
4
). The number of black corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w_{1}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
T
is
6
, which is indeed the same as the number of black corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w_{1}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
T^{\circ}
plus the number of children of
\emptyset
in
T
, and which is less or equal than the number of corners branching to the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w_{1}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
\hat{T^{\circ}}
(which is
5
) plus one.

The first idea is to reduce the statement to a one-type tree, a reduced white subtree. Specifically, we proceed in several elementary steps :

—

For
0\leq j\leq K_{n}
, we denote
v^{\circ,n}_{j}
the
j
-th white vertex of
\mathscr{T}_{n}
in lexicographical order
and
\ell^{\circ,n}_{j}
the number of black corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,v^{\circ,n}_{j}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
.
If
v^{\circ,n}_{j}
is the parent of
v^{\bullet,n}_{i}
, we have
\ell^{\circ,n}_{j}=\ell^{\bullet,n}_{i}
.
We conclude that

\max_{0\leq i<n-K_{n}}\ell^{\bullet,n}_{i}=\max_{0\leq j\leq K_{n}}\ell^{\circ,n}_{j}.

—

Denote by
\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\circ}
the tree obtained by re-rooting
\mathscr{T}_{n}
at its first white corner (see Figure
13
). Then for every white vertex
w
, the number of black corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w[\hskip-1.00006pt[
is the same in
\mathscr{T}_{n}
and in
\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\circ}
(except for white vertices which are descendants of the first child of
\emptyset
in
\mathscr{T}_{n}
, for which one has to add the number of children of
\emptyset
in
\mathscr{T}_{n}
). See Figure
13
for an example.

—

Denote by
\hat{\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\circ}}
the reduced white-subtree of
\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\circ}
. Then for every white vertex
w
, the number of black corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\circ}
is less than or equal to the number of corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
\hat{\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\circ}}
(except for white vertices which are the descendants of the first child of
\emptyset
in
\mathscr{T}_{n}
, for which one has to add
1
). See Figure
13
for an example. The reason why there may not be equality is that the reduction operation forgets the genealogy of black vertices : for instance, if in Figure
13
the vertex
z
were a younger sibling of
w_{2}
, the reduced white subtree would not have changed, but the number of black corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,w[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\circ}
would have increased.

Thanks to the previous observations, it therefore remains to check that

\frac{1}{n}\max_{0\leq i\leq K_{n}}\hat{\ell}^{\circ,n}_{i}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(\mathbb{P})}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0,

where
\hat{\ell}^{\circ,n}_{i}
is the number of corners branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,v^{\circ,n}_{i}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
, where
v^{\circ,n}_{i}
is the
i
-th vertex in lexicographical order of the white reduced tree
\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}}
.

In turn, if
\hat{R}^{\circ,n}_{i}
denotes the number of white
vertices
branching on the right of
[\hskip-1.00006pt[\emptyset,v^{\circ,n}_{i}[\hskip-1.00006pt[
in
\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}}
, we have

\max_{0\leq i\leq K_{n}}\hat{\ell}^{\circ,n}_{i}\leq\mathsf{Height}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}})+\max_{0\leq i\leq K_{n}}\hat{R}^{\circ,n}_{i}.

The advantage of considering
\hat{R}^{\circ,n}_{i}
is that this quantity is simply the value at time
i
of the Łukasiewicz path of
\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}}
(this is a well-known fact of the depth-first search of trees). But Proposition
4.14
, applied with colors exchanged, shows that the Łukasiewicz path of
\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}}
, scaled in time by a factor
n
and in space by a factor
\sqrt{n}
possesses a functional scaling limit. As a consequence,
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{0\leq i\leq K_{n}}\hat{R}^{\circ,n}_{i}
converges in distribution. Therefore, it remains to check that

\frac{\mathsf{Height}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}})}{n}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(\mathbb{P})}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0.

To this end, we will establish that for every fixed
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Height}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}})\geq n^{\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\right)\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad 0,

(69)

which is a stronger result. This is classical (see e.g.
[
BK00
,
MM03
]
) for one-type (critical, finite variance) BGW trees conditioned on having a fixed size. The main issue here is that even though
\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}}
may be seen as a “one-type tree”,
\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}}
is
not
a BGW tree conditioned on having a fixed size (this would have been the case if
\mathscr{T}_{n}
was obtained by conditioning
\mathscr{T}^{n}
on having a fixed number of white vertices only). Another (smaller) issue is that offspring distributions depend on
n
.

Step 2 : Reduction to a non-conditioned statement.

In order to get rid of the conditioning, we start by adapting Section
3.2
in the setting where the root of the two-type tree is white. We fix a tree
\tau
, and we now assume that white vertices are at even generation and black vertices are at odd generation (this is the case of
\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}
). Denote by
(v^{\circ}_{i}(\tau))_{0\leq i<\lvert\circ_{\tau}\rvert}
the white vertices of
\tau
listed in lexicographical order. For
1\leq i\leq\lvert\circ_{\tau}\rvert
, let
H_{i}(\tau)
be the number of black children of
v^{\circ}_{i-1}(\tau)
and let
W_{i}(\tau)
be the number of white grandchildren of
v^{\circ}_{i-1}(\tau)
. Finally, set
\overline{H}_{0}(\tau)=\overline{H}_{0}(\tau)=0
and for
1\leq i\leq\lvert\circ_{\tau}\rvert
set

\overline{H}_{i}(\tau)=H_{1}(\tau)+\cdots+H_{i}(\tau),\qquad\overline{W}_{i}(\tau)=W_{1}(\tau)+\cdots+W_{i}(\tau)-i.

Now note that the tree whose Łukasiewicz path is
\overline{W}(\tau)
is precisely the
reduced white subtree
of
\tau
.
Let
(H^{n},W^{n})
be a random variable such that

\mathbb{P}\left(H^{n}=i,W^{n}=j\right)=\mu_{\circ}^{n}(i)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{i}=j\right),\qquad i,j\geq 0.

(70)

Let
(H^{n}_{i},W^{n}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be a sequence of i.i.d. random variables distributed as
(H^{n},W^{n})
. Set
\overline{H}^{n}_{i}=H^{n}_{1}+H^{n}_{2}+\cdots+H^{n}_{i}
and
\overline{W}^{n}_{i}=W_{1}^{n}+W^{n}_{2}+\cdots+W^{n}_{i}-i
. Then Lemma
3.1
(ii) (applied with colors switched) tells us that
(H_{i}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\circ}),W_{i}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\circ}))_{1\leq i\leq K_{n}+1}
has the same distribution as
(H^{n}_{i},W^{n}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq K_{n}+1}
conditioned on the event

\mathcal{C}^{+}_{n}\coloneqq\{\overline{H}^{n}_{K_{n}+1}=n-K_{n},\overline{W}^{n}_{K_{n}+1}=-1\textrm{ and }\overline{W}^{n}_{i}\geq 0\textrm{ for every }1\leq i<K_{n}+1\}.

We now observe that
u^{\circ}_{i}(\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n})
is an ancestor of
u^{\circ}_{k}(\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n})
in
\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}

(or equivalently in the white reduced tree
\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}}
)
if and only if
\overline{W}_{i}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}})=\min_{i\leq j\leq k}\overline{W}_{j}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}})
.
Therefore the height of
u^{\circ}_{k}(\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n})
in the white reduced tree
\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}}

is equal to
\#\{0\leq i\leq k-1:\overline{W}_{i}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}})=\min_{i\leq j\leq k}\overline{W}_{j}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}})\}
. As a consequence, we can write

\mathsf{Height}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}})=\Psi\big(\overline{W}_{i}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}}):0\leq i\leq K_{n}+1\big),

where for any discrete trajectory
\omega=(\omega_{0},\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{K_{n}+1})
we have set

\Psi(\omega)=\max_{0\leq k\leq K_{n}+1}\#\left\{0\leq i\leq k-1:\omega_{i}=\min_{i\leq j\leq k}\omega_{j}\right\}.

As a consequence, for fixed
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Height}(\hat{\mathscr{T}^{\circ}_{n}})\geq n^{\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\Psi(\overline{W}_{i}:0\leq i\leq K_{n}+1)\geq n^{{1}/{2}+\varepsilon},{\mathcal{C}^{+}_{n}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{n}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\Psi(\overline{W}^{n}_{i}:0\leq i\leq K_{n}+1)\geq n^{{1}/{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{n}\right)}

We now claim that for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
such that for every
n
sufficiently large

\mathbb{P}\big(\Psi(\overline{W}^{n}_{i}:0\leq i\leq K_{n}+1)\geq n^{{1}/{2}+\varepsilon}\big)\leq e^{-n^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}.

(71)

The same reasoning that led to Lemma
4.29
shows that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{n}\right)\sim\tfrac{C}{n^{2}}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, so that the estimate (
) indeed implies (
).

Step 3 : large deviation estimates.

Our goal is now to prove the large deviation estimate (
).
For a discrete trajectory

\omega=(\omega_{0},\dots,\omega_{k})
,
in addition to
\Psi(\omega)
, we also let

\Phi(\omega)=\#\left\{0<i\leq k:\omega_{i}=\max_{0\leq j\leq i}\,\omega_{j}\right\}

be the number of weak records of
\omega
.
By a standard time-reversal argument, we have, for every fixed
k\geq 0
,

\#\left\{0\leq i<k:\overline{W}^{n}_{i}=\min_{i\leq j\leq k}\overline{W}^{n}_{j}\right\}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\quad\Phi(\overline{W}^{n}_{0},\dots,\overline{W}^{n}_{k}).

By definition,
\Psi(\overline{W}^{n}_{i}:0\leq i\leq K_{n}+1)
is the maximum
of the left-hand side of the above display for
k
in
\{0,\dots,K_{n}\}
.
Thus we have

\mathbb{P}\Big(\Psi(\overline{W}^{n}_{i}:0\leq i\leq K_{n}+1)\geq n^{1/2+\varepsilon}\Big)\leq\sum_{0\leq k\leq K_{n+1}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\Phi(\overline{W}^{n}_{i}:0\leq i\leq k)\geq n^{1/2+\varepsilon}\Big)\\
\leq(K_{n}+2)\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\Phi(\overline{W}^{n}_{i}:0\leq i\leq K_{n}+1)\geq n^{1/2+\varepsilon}\Big),

where the second inequality follows form the monotonicity of
\Phi
.
As a consequence, (
) will follow if we establish that
for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
such that for every
n
sufficiently large

\mathbb{P}\big(\Phi(\overline{W}_{i}:0\leq i\leq K_{n}+1)\geq n^{1/2+\varepsilon}\big)\leq e^{-n^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}.

(72)

To this end, we closely follow
[
MM03
]
(see also
[
LG05
, Sec. 1.3]
). Set

\nu^{n}(k)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mu_{\circ}^{n}(i)\mathbb{P}\left(S^{\bullet,n}_{i}=k\right),\qquad k\geq 0,

so that
(\overline{W}^{n}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is a random walk with jump distribution

\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{W}^{n}_{1}=k\right)=\nu^{n}(k+1)
.
Observe that
\nu^{n}
has expectation
1
,
so that
\overline{W}^{n}
is centered.
We denote by
\sigma_{n}^{2}
the variance of
\overline{W}^{n}_{1}
,
which converges to a positive constant when
n
tends to infinity
(since the parameters
a_{\bullet}^{n},b_{\bullet}^{n},a_{\circ}^{n},b_{\circ}^{n}

of the laws
\mu_{\bullet}^{n}
and
\mu_{\circ}^{n}
converge to those of
\mu_{\bullet}
and
\mu_{\circ}
).
Finally we let

T^{n}=\inf\Big\{k\geq 1:\overline{W}^{n}_{k}=\max_{0\leq i\leq k}\overline{W}^{n}_{i}\Big\},\quad M^{n}=\max_{0\leq i\leq{K_{n}+1}}\overline{W}^{n}_{i}.

be respectively the first ladder time and the maximum of
\overline{W}^{n}
up to time
K_{n}+1
.

Observe that the quantity
\mathbb{P}\big(\Phi(\overline{W}_{i}:0\leq i\leq K_{n}+1)\geq n^{1/2+\varepsilon}\big)
is bounded from above by

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\big|M^{n}-\frac{\sigma_{n}^{2}}{2}\Phi(\overline{W}^{n}_{0},\dots,\overline{W}^{n}_{K_{n}+1})\big|\geq n^{1/4+\varepsilon}\bigg)+\mathbb{P}\bigg(M^{n}\geq n^{1/2+\varepsilon}-\frac{2}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}n^{1/4+\varepsilon}\bigg)

(73)

In order to bound from above the first term of (
), we use the fact that there exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that for every
n
sufficiently large

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\big|M^{n}-\frac{\sigma_{n}^{2}}{2}\Phi(\overline{W}^{n}_{0},\dots,\overline{W}^{n}_{K_{n}+1})\big|>n^{1/4+\varepsilon}\bigg)<e^{-n^{\varepsilon_{1}}},

(74)

as in
[
LG05
, Lemma 1.11]
(we warn the reader that in loc. cit.,

\Phi(\overline{W}^{n}_{0},\dots,\overline{W}^{n}_{K_{n}+1})
is denoted by
K_{n}
).
We cannot directly apply
[
LG05
, Lemma 1.11]
because our offspring distributions depend on
n
;
we note however that the same proof can be applied,
provided that we have uniform deviation estimates for sums of i.i.d. variables
distributed as
\overline{W}^{n}_{1}
or
\overline{W}^{n}_{T^{n}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_{n}^{2}
;
here, uniform means that the quantities
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon_{1}
appearing
in the exponents should be independent of
n
.
Such estimates directly follow from the estimate for its Laplace transform,
given in Lemma
4.32
(ii) and (iii) below (see, e.g., the proof of
[
LG05
, Lemma 1.12]
).
Therefore (
) is proved.

In order to bound from above the second term of (
), we use the fact that there exists
\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,\varepsilon/4)
such that for every
n
sufficiently large

\mathbb{P}\bigg(M^{n}>n^{1/2+\varepsilon}-\frac{2}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}n^{1/4+\varepsilon}\bigg)\leq n\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\overline{W}^{n}_{k}>n^{1/2+\varepsilon}-\frac{2}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}n^{1/4+\varepsilon}\bigg)\leq ne^{-n^{\varepsilon_{2}}}.

(75)

This similarly follows the proof of
[
LG05
, Lemma 1.12]
combined with Lemma
4.32
(ii) below.

By combing Eqs. (
) and (
) with (
), we get (
) and this completes the proof.

Lemma 4.32
.

The following assertions hold.

(i)

Let
(Y_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of centered random variables such that there exists
a>0
and
C_{1}>0
such that
\mathbb{E}\left[e^{a|Y_{n}|}\right]\leq C_{1}
for every
n\geq 1
. Then there exists
C_{2}>0
such that for
\lambda>0
sufficiently small, for every
n\geq 1
,
\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda Y_{n}}\big]\leq e^{C_{2}\lambda^{2}}
.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for
\lambda>0
sufficiently small, for every
n\geq 1
,
\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda\overline{W}^{n}_{1}}\big]\leq e^{C\lambda^{2}}
.

(iii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for
\lambda>0
sufficiently small, for every
n\geq 1
,
\mathbb{E}\big[{e^{\lambda(\overline{W}^{n}_{T^{n}}-{\sigma_{n}^{2}}/{2})}}\big]\leq e^{C\lambda^{2}}
.

Proof 4.33
.

The first assertion is a standard result concerning sub-exponential distributions. However, since we need estimates uniform in
n
, we give a proof for completeness. First, using Markov’s inequality, write, for
t\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}(|Y_{n}|\geq t)=\mathbb{P}(e^{a|Y_{n}|}\geq e^{at})\leq C_{1}e^{-at}.

Hence, for
n,k\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}\left[|Y_{n}|^{k}\right]=\int_{0}^{\infty}kt^{k-1}\mathbb{P}\left(|Y_{n}|\geq t\right){{\rm d}}t\leq C_{1}k\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k-1}e^{-at}{{\rm d}}t.

In particular,
\mathbb{E}\left[|Y_{n}|^{2}\right]\leq 2C_{1}/a^{2}
.
Now, for
\lambda>0
small enough, since
\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}]=0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda Y_{n}}\right]=1+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{n}^{k}\right]\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}\leq 1+\frac{C_{1}}{a^{2}}\lambda^{2}+C_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{k=3}^{\infty}kt^{k-1}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}\right)e^{-at}{{\rm d}}t.

By calculating explicitly the last sum and then the integral, we finally get that for
\lambda\in(0,a/2)
, for every
n\geq 1
,
\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda Y_{n}}\big]\leq 1+\tfrac{C_{1}}{a^{2}}\lambda^{2}+\tfrac{2C_{1}}{a^{3}}\lambda^{3}\leq 1+\big(\tfrac{C_{1}}{a^{2}}+\tfrac{2C_{1}}{a^{3}}\big)\lambda^{2}
, which gives the desired result with
C_{2}=\tfrac{C_{1}}{a^{2}}+\tfrac{2C_{1}}{a^{3}}
.

For the second assertion, by (i), it is enough to show that there exists
a>0
and
C_{1}>0
such that
\mathbb{E}\big[e^{a|\overline{W}^{n}_{1}|}\big]\leq C_{1}
for every
n\geq 1
.
Since
\overline{W}^{n}_{1}
takes value in
\{-1,0,1,\dots\}
, it is equivalent to prove
that

\mathbb{E}\big[e^{a(\overline{W}^{n}_{1}+1)}\big]\leq C^{\prime}_{1}
for every
n\geq 1

(where
C^{\prime}_{1}
is another constant).
To this end, write

\mathbb{E}\big[e^{a(\overline{W}^{n}_{1}+1)}\big]\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mu_{\circ}^{n}(i)\mathbb{E}\big[e^{aS^{\bullet,n}_{i}}\big]=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mu_{\circ}^{n}(i)\mathbb{E}\big[e^{aS^{\bullet,n}_{1}}\big]^{i}.

(76)

Let
\delta>0
be such that (
) holds.
Hence
\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{E}[e^{\delta S^{\bullet,n}_{1}}]<\infty

and from the first assertion,
for any
A>1
, we have
\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{E}[e^{aS^{\bullet,n}_{1}}]<A
for
a
sufficiently small.
Choosing
A=e^{\delta}
, and going back to (
), we get

\mathbb{E}\big[e^{a(\overline{W}^{n}_{1}+1)}\big]\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mu_{\circ}^{n}(i)e^{\delta i}.

By (
), the right-hand side is finite and bounded, as a function of
n
,
which concludes the proof of (ii).

For the last assertion, we first observe that by
[
LG05
, Lemma 1.9]
, the law of
\overline{W}^{n}_{T^{n}}
is given by

\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{W}^{n}_{T^{n}}=k\right)=\nu^{n}([k,\infty)),\qquad k\geq 0.

Since
\sigma_{n}^{2}
converges as
n\rightarrow\infty
,
it is enough to show that there exists
a>0
and
C>0

such that
\mathbb{E}\big[e^{a|\overline{W}^{n}_{T^{n}}|}\big]\leq C
for every
n\geq 1
.
Note that the absolute values are here superfluous since
\overline{W}^{n}_{T^{n}}

is nonnegative.

The proof of (ii) shows that there exists two constants
C^{\prime},b>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{W}^{n}_{T^{n}}=k\right)=\nu^{n}([k,\infty))=\mathbb{P}(\overline{W}^{n}_{1}\geq k-1)\leq e^{-b(k-1)}\mathbb{E}\big[e^{b\,\overline{W}^{n}_{1}}\big]\leq C^{\prime}e^{-bk}

for every
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 0
.
This implies the existence of
a>0
and
C>0
such that
\mathbb{E}\big[e^{a\overline{W}^{n}_{T^{n}}}\big]\leq C
for every
n\geq 1
. This concludes the proof.

4.5.
Completing the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

So far, we have established Theorem
1.3
in the following cases :

—

(i)
c=0
only for
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
in Section
2.2
;

—

(i)
c>0
in Section
4.3
;

—

(ii) in Section
4.4
.

In this Section, we treat the missing cases. More precisely, in Section
4.5.1
, we show that the convergence
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
in case (iii) follows from (ii) by a maximality argument. Then, in Section
4.5.2
, we establish, in case (i)
c=0
, the convergence
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{0}
by using, surprisingly, the convergence
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
just established in Section
4.5.1
. We conclude in Section
4.5.3
that the last missing convergence
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{c}
in case (iii) holds by using a short symmetry argument, as well as again the convergence
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
established in Section
4.5.1
.

4.5.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
(iii) for
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
.

Suppose that
\frac{n-K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, with
c\geq 0
. We choose
K^{\prime}_{n}
such that
\frac{K^{\prime}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\frac{n-K^{\prime}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and
K^{\prime}_{n}\leq K_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large. Then by Theorem
1.3
(ii), which has been established in Section
4.4
,
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K^{\prime}_{n}}
converges in distribution to
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
, which is maximal for inclusion. Since
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K^{\prime}_{n}}\subset\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
, it readily follows by a compactness argument that
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
also converges in distribution to
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
(and in fact we have the joint convergence
(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K^{\prime}_{n}},\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\to(Z,Z)
, where
Z
has the distribution of
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
). This completes the proof. ∎

Remark 4.34
.

The same argument can be used to deduce the convergence of
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
to
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
in the case

c=\infty,\,\gamma>0
from the case
c=\infty,\,\gamma=0
.
Since in Section
4.4
, the most difficult part is the case
\gamma>0
,
we could have shortened significantly the proof if we were only interested in
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}

and not in
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
. However, it is not true in general that if
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
, then
(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}},\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\rightarrow(\mathbf{L}_{\infty},\mathbf{L}_{\infty})
(but it is true that if
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
then
(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}},\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\rightarrow(\mathbf{L}_{\infty},\mathbf{L}_{\infty})
, see Lemma
4.15
).

4.5.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
(i)
c=0
for
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
.

Assume that
K_{n}\rightarrow\infty
and that
\frac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\to 0
.
We want to prove that
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
tends to
\mathbb{L}_{0}=\mathbb{S}
.
Recall from Lemma
2.5
that
the blocks of
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
are the connected components of
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
; in particular,
\mathbb{S}\cap\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}=\mathbb{S}\cap\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
).
Since
\mathbb{S}\cap\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
converges to
\mathbb{S}
in distribution
(this was established in the second part of the convergence in distribution

\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{0}
in Section
2.2
),
any limit point of
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
contain
\mathbb{S}
and
it is enough to check that

\Lambda(\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\rightarrow 0
in probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
,
where
\Lambda(L)
is the Euclidean length of the longest chord of the lamination
L
.

In the first part of the proof of the convergence in distribution
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{0}
in Section
2.2
, it was shown that
\Lambda(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\rightarrow 0
in probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
The problem is that
\Lambda(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\rightarrow 0
does not automatically imply that
\Lambda(\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\rightarrow 0
,
since
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
may have a connected component made of many small chords
that creates a long chord in
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
(for instance, if
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}=\cup_{k=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}[e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi k/n},e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi(k+1)/n}]
, then
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
has a chord of length approximately
2
while
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
only has chords of length order
1/n
).

To prove
\Lambda(\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\rightarrow 0
, we shall additionally use the convergence
of
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{0}
(proved in Section
2.2
since we are in the case
c=0
)
and of
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{n-1}
to the Brownian triangulation
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
(proved in Section
4.5.1
).
From Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we can assume that the convergence

\big(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}},\Lambda(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}),\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{n-1}\big)\rightarrow(\mathbb{S},0,\mathbf{L}_{\infty})
holds almost surely.
Note also that, almost surely, for every
\varepsilon>0
, for every arc
{A}\subset\mathbb{S}
of length at least
\varepsilon
,

\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
contains a chord of positive Euclidean length with endpoints belonging to
{A}
(indeed, this easily follows from the fact that local minima times are almost surely dense for the Brownian excursion).

We argue by contradiction, and assume the existence of
\varepsilon>0
such that,
up to extraction,
\Lambda(\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\geq 2\varepsilon
for every
n\geq 1
.
By compactness, up to extraction, we may assume that

\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
contains a chord
[e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi s^{n}},e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi t^{n}}]

such that
s^{n}\rightarrow s
and
t^{n}\rightarrow t
,
with
\min(t-s,1-t+s)\geq\varepsilon
.
Since
\Lambda(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\rightarrow 0
, for
n
sufficiently large
the chord
[e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi s^{n}},e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi t^{n}}]
is not in
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
. But since the blocks of
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
are the connected components of
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
,
ns^{n}
and
nt^{n}
must belong to the same connected component
\mathcal{C}_{n}
of
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
. By considering the path that joins them in
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
, we get the existence of
s_{1}^{n},\ldots,s_{d_{n}}^{n}
with
s_{1}^{n}=s^{n}
,
s_{d_{n}}=t^{n}
, and
ns_{i}^{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
for every
1\leq i\leq d_{n}
, with either
\{s_{1}^{n},\ldots,s_{d_{n}}^{n}\}\subset[s^{n},t^{n}]
, or
\{s_{1}^{n},\ldots,s_{d_{n}}^{n}\}\subset[0,1]\backslash[s^{n},t^{n}]
. To simplify, let us treat the first case (the argument in the second case is exactly the same). Then
s_{1}^{n}\leq s_{2}^{n}\leq\cdots\leq s_{d_{n}}^{n}
, and since
\Lambda(\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})\rightarrow 0
, we have
\max_{1\leq i\leq d_{n}-1}(s^{n}_{i+1}-s_{i}^{n})\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Since
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
is a non-crossing tree, this implies that for every fixed
\eta>0
, for
n
sufficiently large, in
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
there is no chord of Euclidean length at least
\eta
with endpoints belonging to
[s^{n},t^{n}]
. Since
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{n-1}
is still a non-crossing tree obtained from
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
by adding chords, so the latter property also holds for
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{n-1}
(see Figure
14
for an illustration).
But
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{n-1}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
. Therefore, in
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
, there is no chord of positive Euclidean length with endpoints belonging to the arc
\{e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi u}:u\in(s,t)\}
. This is a contradiction, and this completes the proof.
∎

Figure 14.
Illustration of the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i)
c=0
for
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
: the blue “macroscopic” chord belongs to
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
. Since the blocks of
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
are the connected components of
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
and since all the chords of
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
are “small”, there exists a path of such chords (in black) joining the two endpoints of the “macroscopic” chord. It is then not possible to add a new “macroscopic” chord with endpoints belonging to the bold dashed red arc.

4.5.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
(iii) for
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}

Assume that
\frac{n-K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow c
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, with
c\geq 0
.
We first establish that

\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\quad\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\quad\mathbf{L}_{c}.

(77)

By Lemma
4.5
, it suffices to show that
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{n-K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{c}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. If
n-K_{n}\rightarrow\infty
, this follows from Theorem
1.3
(i) for
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{n-K_{n}}
(which was established in Section
4.3
for
c>0
and in Section
4.5.2
for
c=0
). If
\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}(n-K_{n})<\infty
, then
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{n-K_{n}}
has a bounded number of chords, whose Euclidean length, divided by
n
, tends to
0
with probability tending to one as
n\rightarrow\infty
(this was established in Section
2.2
), and this readily implies the convergence
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{n-K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{0}
. This establishes (
).

Now assume that
c>0
. Since
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
is obtained from
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
by adding points of
\mathbb{S}
and since
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{c}
in distribution, any sub-sequential distributional limit of
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
must contain all the chords of
\mathbf{L}_{c}
, and by (
) must be equal to
\mathbf{L}_{c}
. In other words, the convergence
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{c}
holds in distribution.

Finally assume that
c=0
. Since
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
is obtained from
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
by adding points of
\mathbb{S}
, (
) shows that any subsequential distribution limit of
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
is included in
\mathbf{L}_{0}=\mathbb{S}
. Therefore, it is enough to show that the convergence
\mathbb{S}\cap\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
holds in distribution as
n\rightarrow\infty
. To this end, the key idea is to use the fact that
\mathbb{S}\cap\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}=\mathbb{S}\cap\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
. Indeed, by Theorem
1.3
(iii) for
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}
(this was established in Section
4.5.1
),
\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
. By (
),
there exists an endpoint of some non-trivial chords of
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}
arbitrarily close to any fixed point of
\mathbb{S}
,
which implies that
\mathbb{S}\cap\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
in distribution as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and hence
\mathbb{S}\cap\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
in distribution as
n\rightarrow\infty
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.35
.

—

In view of the previous results, we have established that a condensation phenomenon occurs in the dual tree
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{P}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})
when either
K_{n}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow 0
, or
\tfrac{n-K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow 0
. Indeed, when
\tfrac{n-K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow 0
, the fact that the convergence
\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K_{n}}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{S}
holds in distribution indicates that the dual tree
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{P}^{(n)}_{K_{n}})
contains a unique (black) vertex of macroscopic degree (of order
n
) such that the subtrees grafted on it all have size
o(n)
. When
K_{n}\rightarrow\infty
and
\tfrac{K_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\rightarrow 0
, the same phenomenon happens with condensation on a white vertex. It seems difficult to establish these facts directly (such as in
[
AL11
,
Jan12
,
Kor15
]
).

—

If
K\geq 1
is fixed, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, we believe that
(\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(n)}_{K},\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{(n)}_{K})
converge jointly in distribution to the same limit, a collection of
K
i.i.d. uniformly distributed points on
\mathbb{S}
, and that this could be established by using Proposition
1.1
.
